Cyclic Sieving and Cluster Duality of Grassmannian This paper is a contribution to the Special Issue on Cluster Algebras. The full collection is available at https://www.emis.de/journals/SIGMA/cluster-algebras.html
Linhui SHEN and Daping WENG

Abstract
We introduce a decorated configuration space 𝒞 ​ onf n × ​ ( a ) \mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a) with a potential function 𝒲 \mathcal{W} . We prove the cluster duality conjecture of Fock–Goncharov for Grassmannians, that is, the tropicalization of ( 𝒞 ​ onf n × ​ ( a ) , 𝒲 ) \big(\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a),\mathcal{W}\big) canonically parametrizes a linear basis of the homogeneous coordinate ring of the Grassmannian Gr a ⁡ ( n ) \operatorname{Gr}_{a}(n) with respect to the Plücker embedding. We prove that ( 𝒞 ​ onf n × ​ ( a ) , 𝒲 ) \big(\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a),\mathcal{W}\big) is equivalent to the mirror Landau–Ginzburg model of the Grassmannian considered by Eguchi–Hori–Xiong, Marsh–Rietsch and Rietsch–Williams. As an application, we show a cyclic sieving phenomenon involving plane partitions under a sequence of piecewise-linear toggles.



keywords
cluster algebra; cluster duality; mirror symmetry; Grassmannian; cyclic sieving phenomenon

1
Introduction

Throughout we let
a
,
b
be positive integers and let
c
be a non-negative integer. We set
n:=a+b
.

1.1
Cluster duality of Grassmannians

Cluster algebras
are a class of commutative algebras introduced by Fomin and Zelevinsky
[
6
]
.
Their geometric counterparts form a family of log Calabi–Yau varieties called cluster varieties.

A
cluster ensemble
1
1

1

See Appendix
A
for a brief review on cluster ensemble.
is a pair
(\mathscr{A},\mathscr{X})
of cluster varieties associated to an equivalence class of skew-symmetrizable matrices introduced by Fock and Goncharov
[
5
]
. The variety
\mathscr{A}
is equipped with an exceptional class
\{\alpha\}
of coordinate charts called
K_{2}
clusters. A rational function of
\mathscr{A}
is called a
universal Laurent polynomial
if it can be expressed as a Laurent polynomial in every
\alpha
. The ring
{\bf up}(\mathscr{A})
of universal Laurent polynomials of
\mathscr{A}
coincides with the
upper cluster algebra
of
[
1
]
. The variety
\mathscr{X}
is equipped with an exceptional class
\{\chi\}
of coordinate charts called Poisson clusters. Let
{\bf up}(\mathscr{X})
be the ring of universal Laurent polynomials in every
\chi
. The
cluster modular group

\mathcal{G}
is a discrete group acting on
\mathscr{A}
and
\mathscr{X}
that respects the cluster structures.

The Fock–Goncharov cluster duality conjecture
[
5
]
asserts that the ring
{\bf up}(\mathscr{A})
admits a natural basis
\mathcal{G}
-equivariantly paramatrized by the
\mathbb{Z}
-tropical points of
\mathscr{X}
, and vice versa. Cluster duality can be viewed as a manifestation of mirror symmetry between
\mathscr{A}
and
\mathscr{X}

[
16
]
. For example, the cluster duality for moduli spaces of local systems has been investigated in
[
13
,
14
]
.

The present paper focuses on the cluster duality for Grassmannians.

In details, we introduce a pair of spaces, i.e., the
decorated Grassmannian

\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}(n)
and the
decorated configuration space

\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}(a)
, both of which are variants of the Grassmanian
\operatorname{Gr}_{a}(n)
.

The decorated Grassmannian
\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}(n)
is essentially an affine cone over
\operatorname{Gr}_{a}(n)
(see Section
2.1
for its definition). In particular, the coordinate ring of
\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}(n)
coincides with the homogeneous coordinate ring of
\operatorname{Gr}_{a}(n)
in its Plücker embedding. The decorated Grassmannian
\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}(n)
admits a particular divisor, whose complement is an affine variety denoted by
\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}^{\times}(n)
. A result of Scott
[
32
]
implied that the coordinate ring
\mathcal{O}\big(\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}^{\times}(n)\big)
coincides with an upper cluster algebra
{\bf up}(\mathscr{A})
. In this sense,
\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}^{\times}(n)
is naturally equipped with a cluster
K_{2}
structure.

The decorated configuration space
\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}(a)
parametrizes
\operatorname{PGL}(V)
-orbits of
n
-many lines in an
a
-dimensional vector space
V
together with a linear isomorphism between every pair of cyclic neighboring lines. After imposing a consecutive general position condition, we obtain a smooth subvariety
\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)
. We prove that

Theorem 1.1

(Theorem
2.15
)
.

The variety
\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)
is an affine variety and is equipped with a cluster Poisson structure. Its coordinate ring
\mathcal{O}\big(\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)\big)
coincides with the algebra
{\bf up}(\mathscr{X})
of universal Laurent polynomials and therefore
\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)\cong\mathrm{Spec}\left(\mathbf{up}\left(\mathscr{X}\right)\right)
.

The cluster Poisson structure on
\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)
is related to the Poisson structure on the space of
a\times n
matrices studied by Gekhtman and Yakimov
[
10
]
. We also notice similarities between our proof of the affine-ness of
\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)
and techniques used by Morier-Genoud, Ovsienko, and Tabachnikov
[
23
]
in their work on the relation between Grassmannian
\operatorname{Gr}_{3}(n)
and the moduli space of 2-frieze patterns. Further studies should be done on the connection between frieze patterns and Grassmannians in general.

Combining Theorem
2.15
, results from
[
14
]
and
[
35
]
, and the work of Gross, Hacking, Keel, and Kontsevich
[
17
]
, we prove the following theorem in Section
4.1
.

Theorem 1.2
.

The pair
\big(\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}^{\times}(n),\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)\big)
admits a natural cluster ensemble structure. The duality conjecture of Fock–Goncharov holds in this case, that is, the coordinate ring
\mathcal{O}\big(\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}^{\times}(n)\big)
admits a cluster modular group equivariant basis parametrized by the
\mathbb{Z}
-tropical set of
\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)
, and vice versa.

In Section
2
, we introduce several natural functions and maps on
\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}^{\times}(n)
and
\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)
, which are summarized as follows

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{matrix}\text{decorated Grassmannian $\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}^{\times}(n)$},\\
\text{free rescaling $\mathbb{G}_{m}$ action},\\
\text{boundary divisor $D=\bigcup_{i}D_{i}$},\\
\text{action by a maximal torus $T\subset\operatorname{GL}_{n}$},\\
\text{twisted cyclic rotation $C_{a}$}\end{matrix}\right\}\!\leftrightarrow\!\left\{\begin{matrix}\text{decorated configuration space $\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)$},\\
\text{twisted monodromy $P$},\\
\text{potential function $\mathcal{W}=\sum_{i}\vartheta_{i}$},\\
\text{weight map $M\colon\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)\rightarrow T^{\vee}$},\\
\text{cyclic rotation $R$}\end{matrix}\right\}.

We investigate the natural cluster correspondence between the ingredients in the above dictionary. In particular, the potential
\mathcal{W}
exhibits an explicit cyclic symmetry. It is essentially equivalent to the potential of the Grassmannian considered in
[
2
,
21
,
29
]
. We identify
\mathcal{W}
with the sum of theta functions associated to frozen vertices under the framework of
[
17
]
. As a consequence, we prove the following.

Theorem 1.3

(Corollaries
4.12
and
4.17
)
.

Within the basis of
\mathcal{O}\big(\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}^{\times}(n)\big)
stated in Theorem
1.2
, the subset
\big\{\theta_{q}\,|\,\mathcal{W}^{t}(q)\geq 0\big\}
is a basis of
\mathcal{O}(\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}(n))
. Moreover, this basis of
\mathcal{O}\left(\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}(n)\right)
is compatible with both the decomposition
\mathcal{O}(\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}(n))\cong\bigoplus_{c\geq 0}V_{c\omega_{a}}
into irreducible
\operatorname{GL}_{n}
-representations as well as the weight space decomposition with respect to a maximal torus
T\subset\operatorname{GL}_{a}
.

Marsh and Rietsch constructed a
B
-model for the Grassmannian in
[
21
]
, which is of the form
\big(\operatorname{Gr}_{a}^{\times}(n)\times\mathbb{G}_{m},\mathcal{W}_{q}\big)
. Rietsch and Williams proved in
[
29
]
that this
B
-model is an example of a cluster dual space of the Grassmannian. In contrast to their approach, our approach is more geometric and is purely motivated by the associated cluster structures. We include a section in the appendix describing the connection between our version of cluster duality and the version considered by Rietsch and Williams.

1.2
Cyclic sieving phenomenon of plane partitions

Let
S
be a finite set and let
g
be a permutation of
S
of order
n
. We are interested in the size of
the fixed point set
S^{g^{d}}
of
g^{d}
for
d\geq 0
. Let
F(q)
be a polynomial of positive integral coefficients and let
\zeta={\rm e}^{2\pi\sqrt{-1}/n}
. Following Reiner–Stanton–White
[
27
]
, we make the following definition.

Definition 1.4
.

We say that the triple
(S,g,F(q))
exhibits the
cyclic sieving phenomenon
(
CSP
)
if the fixed point set cardinality
\#S^{g^{d}}
is equal to the polynomial evaluation
F\big(\zeta^{d}\big)
for all
d\geq 0
.

Many combinatorial models have been found to exhibit the cyclic sieving phenomenon. Interestingly, the proofs often involve deep results in representation theory. For example, Rhoades
[
28
]
proved the CSP for rectangular Young tableaux under the action of promotion, by using Kazhdan–Lusztig theory and a representation of the Hecke algebra.
In
[
7
]
, Fontaine and Kamnizter studied the CSP for minuscule Littelmann paths under rotation using the geometric Satake correspondence and intersection homology of quiver varieties.
For more examples, we refer the interested reader to a survey on this topic by Sagan
[
31
]
.

As an application of our result on cluster duality for Grassmannian, we prove the CSP for plane partitions under a sequence of piecewise-linear toggles.

Definition 1.5
.

A size
a\times b
plane partition is an
a\times b
matrix
\pi=\left(\pi_{i,j}\right)
of non-negative integer entries
\pi_{i,j}
that is weakly decreasing in rows and columns, and for which we define

|\pi|:=\sum_{i,j}\pi_{i,j}.

Denote by
P(a,b,c)
the set of size
a\times b
plane partitions with largest entry
\pi_{1,1}\leq c
.

Let
[m]_{q}
denote the quantum integer
\frac{1-q^{m}}{1-q}.

MacMahon’s formula asserts that

M_{a,b,c}(q):=\sum_{\pi\in P(a,b,c)}q^{|\pi|}=\prod_{i=1}^{a}\prod_{j=1}^{b}\prod_{k=1}^{c}\frac{\left[i+j+k-1\right]_{q}}{\left[i+j+k-2\right]_{q}}.

Following
[
30
, Section 4]
, we consider
piecewise-linear toggles
on
P(a,b,c)
.
Let
\pi\in P(a,b,c)
. Let
1\leq i\leq a
and let
1\leq j\leq b
. We make the convention that
\pi_{0,j}=\pi_{i,0}=c
and
\pi_{a+1,j}=\pi_{i,b+1}=0
. The piecewise-linear toggle
\tau_{i,j}
at the
(i,j)
-th entry is an involution that sends
\pi
to a new plane partition
\tau_{i,j}\pi
such that

(\tau_{i,j}\pi)_{k,l}:=\begin{cases}\pi_{k,l}&\text{if $(k,l)\neq(i,j)$},\\
\max\left\{\pi_{i,j+1},\pi_{i+1,j}\right\}+\min\left\{\pi_{i-1,j},\pi_{i,j-1}\right\}-\pi_{i,j}&\text{if $(k,l)=(i,j)$}.\end{cases}

(1.1)

A birational version of
\tau_{i,j}
has been constructed by Einstein and Propp in
[
3
]
and by Musiker and Roby in
[
24
]
. See also
[
14
, Section 9.3]
for the same construction of the birational Schützenberger involution of Gelfand–Tsetlin patterns.

Let
\eta
be the sequence of piecewise-linear toggles that hits each entry exactly once in the order from bottom to top and from left to right, that is,

\eta=\nu_{b}\circ\nu_{b-1}\circ\cdots\circ\nu_{1},\qquad\mbox{where}\quad\nu_{j}=\tau_{1,j}\circ\tau_{2,j}\circ\cdots\circ\tau_{a,j}.

For example, applying
\eta
to
\left(\begin{smallmatrix}3&2&2\\
3&1&0\end{smallmatrix}\right)\in P(2,3,6)

yields

3
2
2
3
1
0
3
2
2
1
1
0
5
2
2
1
1
0
5
2
2
1
0
0
5
5
2
1
0
0
5
5
2
1
0
0
5
5
3
1
0
0
\tau_{2,1}
\tau_{1,1}
\tau_{2,2}
\tau_{1,2}
\tau_{2,3}
\tau_{1,3}

Applying the cluster duality of Grassmannians, we obtain the following result.

Theorem 1.6
.

The action
\eta
on
P(a,b,c)
is of order
n=a+b
. The triple
(P(a,b,c),\eta,M_{a,b,c}(q))
exhibits a cyclic sieving phenomenon.

The proof of Theorem
1.6
involves representations of
{\rm GL}_{n}
. Recall that irreducible finite dimensional representations of
{\rm GL}_{n}
are parametrized by weakly decreasing sequences,
\mu_{1}\geq\dots\geq\mu_{n}
, with all
\mu_{i}\in\mathbb{Z}
. The
a
th fundamental weight
\omega_{a}:=(\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{n})
takes the form
\mu_{1}=\dots=\mu_{a}=1
and
\mu_{a+1}=\dots=\mu_{n}=0
. Let
V_{c\omega_{a}}
be the irreducible representation of
\operatorname{GL}_{n}
with
c\omega_{a}
as its highest weight. We define the
twisted cyclic rotation
on
V_{c\omega_{a}}
to be the action of

C_{a}:=\begin{pmatrix}0&(-1)^{a-1}\\
\text{Id}_{n-1}&0\end{pmatrix}\in\operatorname{GL}_{n}.

(1.2)

Theorem
1.6
is then an easy consequence of the following result.

Theorem 1.7
.

The basis of
V_{c\omega_{a}}
obtained in Theorem
1.3
is in natural bijection with
P(a,b,c)
. The bijection is equivariant with respect to the twisted cyclic rotation
C_{a}
on
V_{c\omega_{a}}
and the sequence of toggles
\eta
.

After the first version of this paper was posted on arXiv, Hopkins
[
18
]
proved that our Theorem
1.6
is equivalent to a result of Rhoades
[
28
, Theorem 1.4]
. In detail, this paper investigates the model of plane partitions under toggles, while Rhoades studied the model of semistandard tableaux under promotion. In Appendix A of
[
18
]
, these two models are shown to be equivariantly equivalent to each other. However, we would like to emphasize that our approach uses Fock–Goncharov’s cluster duality of Grassmannian, which is new and is significantly different from Rhoades’s approach of using Kazhdan–Lusztig theory. In particular, we present a geometric interpretation of the plane partitions under toggles, i.e., they are the tropicalization of decorated configurations under rotation. It follows immediately from the latter that the order of the toggle action is
n
. In general, the framework of cluster duality can potentially be applied to proving CSP involving other types of cluster varieties.

2
Main definitions

2.1
Decorated Grassmannian

Let
V
be an
n
-dimensional vector space and let
V^{*}
be its dual.

Definition 2.1
.

The
decorated Grassmannian

\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}(V^{*})
is a space which parametrizes the pairs
(W^{*},f^{*})
, where
W^{*}
is an
a
-dimensional subspace of
V^{*}
and
f^{*}\in\bigwedge^{a}W^{*}
is a non-zero
a
-form.

These non-zero
a
-forms
f^{*}
are also known as the
decomposable
elements of the exterior power
\bigwedge^{a}V^{*}
because they can be written as a single exterior product. Therefore decorated Grassmannian
\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}(V^{*})
naturally sits inside the exterior power
\bigwedge^{a}V^{*}
as a quasi-affine variety.

There is a free right
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-action on
\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}(V^{*})
defined via rescaling the
a
-forms

(W^{*},f^{*}).t:=(W^{*},tf^{*}).

(2.1)

This action coincides with the rescaling action on
\bigwedge^{a}V^{*}
. If we projectivize the inclusion of the decorated Grassmannian
\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}(V^{*})\hookrightarrow\bigwedge^{a}V^{*}
with respect to this action, we recover the Plücker embedding of the ordinary Grassmannian
\operatorname{Gr}_{a}(V^{*})\hookrightarrow\mathbb{P}\big(\bigwedge^{a}V^{*}\big)
.
Recall that the affine cone of the ordinary Grassmannian
\operatorname{Gr}_{a}(V^{*})
in
its Plücker embedding is the affine subvariety of decomposable
a
-forms in
\bigwedge^{a}V^{*}
.
Therefore
\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}(V^{*})
is isomorphic to the affine cone deleting the point
0\in\bigwedge^{a}V^{*}
.
This is why, throughout the paper, we sometimes refer to the ring of regular functions
\mathcal{O}(\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}(V^{*}))
as a homogeneous coordinate ring of the ordinary Grassmannian
\operatorname{Gr}_{a}(V^{*})
.

The right
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-action on
\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}(V^{*})
induces a left
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-action on
\mathcal{O}(\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}(V^{*}))
. Irreducible representations of
\mathbb{G}_{m}
are 1-dimensional and are classified by integers. Denote by
\mathcal{O}(\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}(V^{*}))_{c}
the eigenspace in
\mathcal{O}(\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}(V^{*}))
that is of weight
c
with respect to the
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-action.

By the natural pairing between
\bigwedge^{a}V^{*}
and
\bigwedge^{a}V
, every
g\in\bigwedge^{a}V
gives rise to a regular function

\Delta_{g}\colon\ \mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}(V^{*})\longrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{1},\qquad\Delta_{g}(W^{*},f^{*}):=\left\langle f^{*},g\right\rangle.

The ring
\mathcal{O}(\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}(V^{*}))
is generated by
\Delta_{g}
modulo certain homogeneous relations called
Plücker relations
((
2.3
); see also, e.g.,
[
15
, p. 211]
for more details). Note that under the
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-action, we have

(t.\Delta_{g})(W^{*},f^{*})=\Delta_{g}(W^{*},tf^{*})=\Delta_{tg}(W^{*},f^{*})=t\Delta_{g}(W^{*},f^{*}).

Since the Plücker relations are homogeneous, the weight space decomposition of
\mathcal{O}(\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}(V^{*}))
with respect to the
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-action coincides with the decomposition into subspaces of homogeneous degrees of the generators
\Delta_{g}
. Therefore we conclude the following statement.

Proposition 2.2
.

The map
g\mapsto\Delta_{g}
is an isomorphism between
\bigwedge^{a}V
and
\mathcal{O}\left(\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}(V^{*})\right)_{1}
, and

\mathcal{O}(\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}(V^{*}))=\bigoplus_{c\geq 0}\mathcal{O}(\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}(V^{*}))_{c}.

(2.2)

The group
\operatorname{GL}(V)
acts on
V
as well as its dual space
V^{*}
, and hence on the decorated Grassmannian
\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}(V^{*})
and on the ring
\mathcal{O}(\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}(V^{*}))
. In particular, the irreducible representation
V_{\omega_{a}}
of
{\rm GL}(V)
can be constructed as
\bigwedge^{a}V
(see, e.g.,
[
8
, Lecture 15]
) and hence
V_{\omega_{a}}
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{O}(\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}(V^{*}))_{1}
by Proposition
2.2
.
In general, by the Borel–Weil theorem, equation (
2.2
) is the decomposition of
\mathcal{O}(\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}(V^{*}))
into irreducible representations of
\operatorname{GL}(V)
with

\mathcal{O}(\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}(V^{*}))_{c}\cong V_{c\omega_{a}}.

From now on, we fix a basis
\{e_{1},\dots,e_{n}\}
of
V
and identify
V
with the vector space
\mathds{k}^{n}
, where
\mathds{k}
is the base field. We abbreviate
\operatorname{Gr}_{a}(V^{*})
to
\operatorname{Gr}_{a}(n)
and
\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}(V^{*})
to
\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}(n)
. Every
a
-element subset
I=\{i_{1},\dots,i_{a}\}\in\binom{[n]}{a}
gives rise to a regular function
\Delta_{I}:=\Delta_{e_{I}}
on
\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}(n)
, where
e_{I}
denotes the wedge product of vectors
e_{i_{1}},\dots,e_{i_{a}}
taken in ascending order (e.g.,
\Delta_{\{5,7,4\}}:=\Delta_{e_{4}\wedge e_{5}\wedge e_{7}}
). The functions
\Delta_{I}
are also known as the
Plücker coordinates
.

The Plücker coordinates of
\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}(n)
satisfy a set of homogeneous relations called
Plücker relations
, and they are generated by 3-term homogeneous quadratic equations of the following form: for any
(a-2)
-element subset
J\subset\{1,\dots,n\}
and any four distinct elements
i,j,k,l\in\{1,\dots,n\}\setminus J
with
i<j<k<l
, the corresponding Plücker relation is

\Delta_{J\cup\{i,j\}}\Delta_{J\cup\{k,l\}}+\Delta_{J\cup\{i,l\}}\Delta_{J\cup\{j,k\}}=\Delta_{J\cup\{i,k\}}\Delta_{J\cup\{j,l\}}.

(2.3)

Let
D_{i}
be the vanishing locus of the Plücker coordinate
\Delta_{\{i,i+1,\dots,{i+a-1}\}}
(indices taken modulo
n
). Let
\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}^{\times}(n)
denote the complement of
D:=\cup_{i=1}^{n}D_{i}
.
Since
\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}^{\times}(n)\subset\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}(n)
, we have
\mathcal{O}\left(\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}(n)\right)\subset\mathcal{O}\big(\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}^{\times}(n)\big)
.
The image of
D
under the projection from
\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}(n)
to
\operatorname{Gr}_{a}(n)
is an anticanonical divisor of
\operatorname{Gr}_{a}(n)

[
21
, equation (19.3)]
. We denote the complement of
D
by
\operatorname{Gr}_{a}^{\times}(n)
.

Let
\operatorname{Mat}_{a,n}^{\times}
be the space of
a\times n
matrices with column vectors
v_{i}
such that every collection
\{v_{i},v_{i+1},\dots,v_{i+a-1}\}
of
a
-many cyclically consecutive column vectors is linearly independent. The group
{\rm SL}_{a}
acts freely on
\operatorname{Mat}_{a,n}^{\times}
by matrix multiplication on the left.

Lemma 2.3
.

The space
\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}^{\times}(n)
is canonically isomorphic to the quotient space
\operatorname{SL}_{a}\backslash\operatorname{Mat}_{a,n}^{\times}
as algebraic varieties.

Proof.

Let
(W^{*},f^{*})\in\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}^{\times}(n)
and let
W
be the dual space of
W^{*}
. Every subspace
W^{*}\subset(\mathds{k}^{n})^{\ast}
naturally induces a surjection
\pi\colon\mathds{k}^{n}\rightarrow W
. Let
v_{i}:=\pi(e_{i})
be the image of the basis element
e_{i}
under
\pi
. The coordinate
\Delta_{i+1,\dots,i+a}\neq 0
is equivalent to the linear independence of
\{v_{i+1},\dots,v_{i+a}\}
. Up to the action of
{\rm SL}_{a}
on
\mathds{k}^{a}
, there is a unique choice of linear isomorphisms from
W
to
\mathds{k}^{a}
whose induced pull-back map maps the standard
a
-form on
\mathds{k}^{a}
(which is an element of
\bigwedge^{a}(\mathds{k}^{a})^{*}
) to the
a
-form
f^{*}
on
W^{*}
. Hence we get a configuration in
{\rm SL}_{a}\backslash\operatorname{Mat}_{a,n}^{\times}
. It is easy to see that such a map is bijective.
∎

Remark 2.4
.

Under the above isomorphism, the coordinates
\Delta_{I}
are identified with the minors of
I
-columns in an
a\times n
matrix.

Let
T=(\mathbb{G}_{m})^{n}
be the maximal torus of
\operatorname{GL}_{n}
consisting of invertible diagonal matrices. It acts on the right of
\operatorname{Mat}_{a,n}^{\times}
by rescaling the column vectors
v_{1},\dots,v_{n}
. Since
\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}^{\times}(n)\cong\operatorname{SL}_{a}\backslash\operatorname{Mat}_{a,n}^{\times}
, the
T
-action on
\operatorname{Mat}_{a,n}^{\times}
descends to a
T
-action on the decorated Grassmannian
\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}^{\times}(n)
.

Define the linear transformation
C_{a}
on
V
such that

C_{a}(e_{i}):=\begin{cases}e_{i-1}&\text{if $i\neq 1$},\\
(-1)^{a-1}e_{n}&\text{if $i=1$}.\end{cases}

It induces a twisted cyclic rotation on
\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}(n)
still denoted by
C_{a}
.

To summarize, we obtain the following data

\begin{cases}\text{the decorated grassmannian $\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}^{\times}(n)$},\\
\text{the free $\mathbb{G}_{m}$-action on $\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}^{\times}(n)$ by rescaling the $a$-form},\\
\text{the boundary divisor $D=\bigcup_{i}D_{i}$},\\
\text{the $T$-action on $\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}^{\times}(n)$},\\
\text{the twisted cyclic rotation $C_{a}$ on $\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}^{\times}(n)$}.\end{cases}

(2.4)

2.2
Decorated configuration space

Let
W
be a vector space of dimension
a
.

Definition 2.5
.

The
configuration space

\operatorname{Conf}_{n}(W)
parametrizes the
\operatorname{PGL}(W)
-orbits of
n
many (not necessarily distinct) lines in
W
, i.e.,

\operatorname{Conf}_{n}(W):=\operatorname{PGL}(W)\left\backslash\left(\prod_{n}\mathbb{P}W\right)\right..

As in Fig.
1
, a
decorated configuration
2
2

2

The definition of decorated configuration is motivated by an idea of A.B. Goncharov on pinnings; see also
[
12
, Section 2.2]
for more details.
is a
\operatorname{PGL}(W)
-orbit of

n
lines in
W
together with linear isomorphisms
\phi_{i}\colon l_{i}\rightarrow l_{i-1}
for each pair of neighboring lines. The
decorated configuration space
is

\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}(W):=\operatorname{PGL}(W)\left\backslash\left\{\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\text{1-dimensional subspaces $l_{1},\dots,l_{n}\subset W$}\\
\text{and linear isomorphisms $\phi_{i}\colon l_{i}\rightarrow l_{i-1}$}\end{array}\right\}.\right.

We denote a decorated configuration as
[\phi_{1},l_{1},\dots,\phi_{n},l_{n}]
.
We frequently omit the subscript of
\phi_{i}
.

\bullet
l_{1}
\bullet
l_{2}
\bullet
l_{3}
\bullet
l_{4}
\bullet
l_{5}
\bullet
l_{6}
\phi_{1}
\phi_{2}
\phi_{3}
\phi_{4}
\phi_{5}
\phi_{6}

Figure 1:
A decorated configuration in
\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{6}(W)
.

Two vector spaces of the same dimension are isomorphic up to choices of bases. Since the action of
\operatorname{PGL}(W)
has been quotiented out, the configuration spaces of
n
lines in vector spaces of the same dimension are canonically isomorphic to each other. Therefore we may abbreviate
\operatorname{Conf}_{n}(W)
to
\operatorname{Conf}_{n}(a)
. For the same reason, we may abbreviate
\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}(W)
to
\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}(a)
.

Let
\operatorname{Conf}^{\times}_{n}(a)
be the subspace of
\operatorname{Conf}_{n}(a)
consisting of configurations
[l_{1},\dots,l_{n}]
such that every collection
\{l_{i+1},\dots,l_{i+a}\}
of
a
-many cyclically consecutive lines is a linearly independent set of lines. The subspace
\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}^{\times}_{n}(a)
of
\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}(a)
is defined in the same way.

Let
[\phi_{1},l_{1},\dots,\phi_{n},l_{n}]\in\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)
.
Let us compose
\phi
in anti-clockwise order as in Fig.
1
. Let
(-1)^{a-1}P
be the
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-valued rescaling factor of the automorphism
\phi_{i+1}\circ\cdots\circ\phi_{n}\circ\phi_{1}\circ\cdots\circ\phi_{i}
on
l_{i}
. Note that
P
is independent of the initial index
i
chosen. We get a projection map called
twisted monodromy

P\colon\ \mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)\longrightarrow\mathbb{G}_{m}.

(2.5)

Proposition 2.6
.

The space
\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}^{\times}_{n}(a)
is an affine variety of dimension
a(n-a)+1
.

Proof.

Pick a non-zero vector
v_{n}\in l_{n}
. Using the maps
\phi_{i}
recursively, we get
v_{i-1}:=\phi_{i}\left(v_{i}\right)\in l_{i-1}
for
i=n,n-1,\dots,2
. From the consecutive general position condition we know that for any
1\leq i\leq n
,
(v_{i},v_{i-1},\dots,v_{i-a+1})
is a basis of
\mathds{k}^{a}
. Define
\Phi_{i}
to be the
a\times a
matrix such that

\begin{pmatrix}v_{i-1}\\
\vdots\\
v_{i-a}\end{pmatrix}=\Phi_{i}\begin{pmatrix}v_{i}\\
\vdots\\
v_{i-a+1}\end{pmatrix}.

(2.6)

Then
\Phi_{i}
must take the form

\Phi_{i}=\begin{pmatrix}0&1&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&\cdots&1\\
*&*&\cdots&*\end{pmatrix}.

Note that
\Phi_{i}
does not depend on the choice of
v_{n}\in l_{n}
. Moreover, since the monodromy
\phi^{n}=(-1)^{a-1}P
, the product
\Phi_{1}\Phi_{2}\cdots\Phi_{n}
should be the identity matrix multiplied by
(-1)^{a-1}P
. Therefore the configuration space
\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)
satisfies the equation

\Phi_{1}\Phi_{2}\cdots\Phi_{n}=\left((-1)^{a-1}P\right)\mathrm{Id}_{a\times a}

(2.7)

with
a
variables in each matrix
\Phi_{i}
and
P
a non-zero parameter. Conversely, one can construct a configuration in
\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)
from any solution to equation (
2.7
) using the matrices
\Phi_{i}
. Therefore we conclude that
\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)
is the intersection of the vanishing loci of
Pt-1
and the functions that are entries of
\Phi_{1}\Phi_{2}\cdots\Phi_{n}-\big((-1)^{a-1}P\big)\mathrm{Id}_{a\times a}
in the affine space
\mathbb{A}^{na}_{\Phi}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}_{P}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}_{t}
. In particular, this shows that the map
P
is regular on
\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)
.

The dimension of
\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}^{\times}_{n}(a)
is

\displaystyle\dim\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}^{\times}_{n}(a)

\displaystyle=n\dim\big(\mathbb{P}^{a-1}\big)+\dim\{\text{isomorphisms $\phi_{i}$}\}-\dim\operatorname{PGL}_{a}

\displaystyle=n(a-1)+n-\big(a^{2}-1\big)=a(n-a)+1.

∎

Below we introduce three natural maps from
\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)
. Let
[\phi_{1},l_{1},\dots,\phi_{n},l_{n}]\in\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)
. Pick a non-zero vector
v_{i}\in l_{i}
for
i=1,\dots,n
.

Because of the cyclically consecutive general position condition, the vector space quotient
W/\mathrm{Span}\{l_{i-a+2},\dots,l_{i}\}
is 1-dimensional and is spanned by
\overline{v_{i-a+1}}
(the image of
v_{i-a+1}
under this quotient). Let
\vartheta_{i}
be the scalar such that in the quotient space
W/\mathrm{Span}\{l_{i-a+2},\dots,l_{i}\}
,

\overline{\phi(v_{i-a+1})}=\vartheta_{i}\overline{v_{i-a+1}}.

(2.8)

Note that
\vartheta_{i}
is independent of the choices of
v_{i}
.

Note that for
i\neq a
,
\vartheta_{i}
is the
(a,a)
-entry of the matrix
\Phi_{i}
defined in equation (
2.6
); for
i=a
,
\vartheta_{a}
is the product of the
(a,a)
-entry of
\Phi_{a}
and
(-1)^{a-1}P
. Since the function
P
and the entries of
\Phi_{i}
are all regular functions on
\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)
, the functions
\vartheta_{i}
are also regular on
\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)
for all
i
.

The
potential function
on
\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}^{\times}_{n}(a)
is defined to be the regular function

\mathcal{W}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\vartheta_{i}\colon\ \mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)\longrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{1}.

(2.9)

Since the top exterior power
\bigwedge^{a}W
is 1-dimensional, under the cyclically consecutive general position condition we may define

M_{k}:=\frac{\phi\left(v_{k-a+1}\right)\wedge\dots\wedge\phi\left(v_{k}\right)}{v_{k-a+1}\wedge\dots\wedge v_{k}}.

(2.10)

Note that the value
M_{k}
is nonzero and does not depend on the choices of
v_{i}
. Therefore we obtain a
weight
map

\displaystyle M\colon\ \mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow T^{\vee},

\displaystyle[\phi_{1},l_{1},\dots,\phi_{n},l_{n}]

\displaystyle\longmapsto(M_{1},\dots,M_{n}),

(2.11)

where
T^{\vee}\cong(\mathbb{G}_{m})^{n}
is the dual torus of the maximal torus
T\subset\operatorname{GL}_{n}
.

Lastly, there is an order
n
biregular map

\displaystyle R\colon\ \mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a),

\displaystyle[\phi_{1},l_{1},\phi_{2},l_{2},\dots,\phi_{n},l_{n}]

\displaystyle\longmapsto[\phi_{n},l_{n},\phi_{1},l_{1},\dots,\phi_{n-1},l_{n-1}].

To summarize, we get the following data

\begin{cases}\text{the decorated configuration space $\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)$},\\
\text{the twisted monodromy $P\colon\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{a}^{\times}(n)\rightarrow\mathbb{G}_{m}$},\\
\text{the potential function $\mathcal{W}=\sum_{i}\vartheta_{i}$},\\
\text{the weight map $M\colon\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)\rightarrow T^{\vee}$},\\
\text{the cyclic rotation $R$ on $\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(n)$}.\end{cases}

2.3
Maps among the decorated spaces

Recall the
n
-dimensional vector space
V
with a basis
\{e_{1},\dots,e_{n}\}
. Let
\hat{l}_{i}
be the line spanned by
e_{i}
and let
\hat{\phi}_{i}\colon\hat{l}_{i}\rightarrow\hat{l}_{i-1}
be the linear isomorphism such that

\hat{\phi}_{i}\left(e_{i}\right):=\begin{cases}e_{i-1}&\text{if $i\neq 1$},\\
(-1)^{a-1}e_{n}&\text{if $i=1$}.\end{cases}

(2.12)

As mentioned in Section
2.1
, the cyclically consecutive Plücker coordinates
\Delta_{\{i,\dots,i+a-1\}}
, with indices taken modulo
n
, cut out an anti-canonical divisor on
\operatorname{Gr}_{a}(V^{*})
, whose complement is denoted by

\operatorname{Gr}_{a}^{\times}(V^{*})
. Every
W^{*}\in\operatorname{Gr}_{a}^{\times}(V^{*})
induces a projection
\pi
from
V
to the dual space
W
of
W^{*}
, and since
\Delta_{\{i,\dots,i+a-1\}}(W^{*})\neq 0
for all
i
, the image of every
e_{i}
under
\pi
is a non-zero vector
v_{i}
, and the lines
l_{i}:=\pi(\hat{l}_{i})
in
W
automatically satisfy the consecutive general position condition. Furthermore, isomorphisms
\hat{\phi}_{i}
descend to isomorphisms
\phi_{i}\colon l_{i}\rightarrow l_{i-1}
. Thus we obtain
[\phi_{1},l_{1},\dots,\phi_{n},l_{n}]\in\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(W)\cong\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)
. This defines a natural map

\displaystyle\operatorname{Gr}_{a}^{\times}(n)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a),

\displaystyle W^{*}

\displaystyle\longmapsto[\phi_{1},l_{1},\dots,\phi_{n},l_{n}].

(2.13)

Proposition 2.7
.

The map (
2.13
) is injective. The image of (
2.13
) consists of decorated configurations of twisted monodromy
P=1
.

Proof.

By (
2.12
), every point in the image of (
2.13
) is of twisted monodromy
P=1
.

Let
[\phi_{1},l_{1},\dots,\phi_{n},l_{n}]
be a configuration in
\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}^{\times}_{n}(a)
with
P=1
.
It remains to show that there is a unique
W^{\ast}\in{\operatorname{Gr}}_{a}^{\times}(V^{\ast})
whose image under the map (
2.13
) is
[\phi_{1},l_{1},\dots,\phi_{n},l_{n}]
.
Let
(\phi_{1},l_{1},\dots,\phi_{n},l_{n})
be a representative of the configuration
[\phi_{1},l_{1},\dots,\phi_{n},l_{n}]
.
Pick a non-zero vector
v_{n}\in l_{n}
and then use the isomorphisms
\phi_{n},\dots,\phi_{2}
to get non-zero vectors
v_{i}\in l_{i}
. It gives rise to a linear projection
\pi\colon V\rightarrow\mathds{k}^{a}
which sends
e_{i}\mapsto v_{i}
. The dual of this map determines an embedding
\pi^{*}\colon\mathds{k}^{a}\hookrightarrow V^{*}
, whose image is denoted as
W^{*}
. Note that changing the choice of representative and the choice of
v_{n}
corresponds to postcomposing the projection
\pi\colon V\rightarrow\mathds{k}^{a}
with an element of
\operatorname{GL}_{a}
. It corresponds to precomposing the dual map
\pi^{\ast}\colon\mathds{k}^{a}\hookrightarrow V^{*}
with an element of
\operatorname{GL}_{a}
, which does not effect the image
W^{*}
of
\pi^{\ast}
as a subspace of
V^{*}
.
∎

Definition 2.8
.

Denote by
\widetilde{\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)}
the space of
{\rm SL}_{a}
-orbits of

(\phi_{1},v_{1},\phi_{2},v_{2},\dots,\phi_{n},v_{n}),

where
v_{i}
are vectors in
\mathds{k}^{a}
satisfying consecutive general position condition, and each
\phi_{i}
is a linear isomorphism from the line spanning
v_{i}
to the line spanning
v_{i-1}
, and
\operatorname{SL}_{a}
acts on
(\phi_{1},v_{1},\dots,\phi_{n},v_{n})
by

g.(\phi_{1},v_{1},\dots,\phi_{n},v_{n}):=\big(g\phi_{1}g^{-1},gv_{1},\dots,g\phi_{n}g^{-1},gv_{n}\big).

By Lemma
2.3
, we can think of the space
\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}^{\times}(n)
as the moduli space of configurations of vectors
[v_{1},\dots,v_{n}]
satisfying the cyclic general position condition. Define the following map

\displaystyle\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}^{\times}(n)

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\widetilde{\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)},

\displaystyle[v_{1},\dots,v_{n}]

\displaystyle\longmapsto[\phi_{1},v_{1},\dots,\phi_{n},v_{n}],

with the isomorphisms
\phi
defined by
\phi_{1}(v_{1}):=(-1)^{a-1}v_{n}
and
\phi_{i}(v_{i}):=v_{i-1}
for the other
i
’s. It is not hard to see that this map is injective.

There is a surjective map
\widetilde{\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)}\rightarrow\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)
by replacing each
v_{i}
by its spanning line
l_{i}
.

There is a surjective map
\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)\rightarrow\operatorname{Conf}_{n}^{\times}(a)
defined by forgetting the isomorphisms
\phi_{i}
.

There is a surjective map
\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}^{\times}(n)\rightarrow\operatorname{Gr}_{a}^{\times}(n)
defined by forgetting the
a
-form.

Proposition 2.9
.

Putting all the aforementioned maps together, we obtain commutative diagram

\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}^{\times}(n)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a).\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Conf}_{n}^{\times}(a)}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Gr}_{a}^{\times}(n)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}

Proof.

It remains to prove the commutativity of the rhombus and the surjectivity of the map
\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}^{\times}(n)\twoheadrightarrow\operatorname{Conf}^{\times}_{n}(a)
.

First, by Lemma
2.3
, we can again view
\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}^{\times}(n)
as moduli space of configurations of vectors
[v_{1},\dots,v_{n}]
in
\mathds{k}^{a}
satisfying cyclic general position condition. But these
v_{i}
are precisely the vectors that span the lines
l_{i}
in the construction of the map
\operatorname{Gr}_{a}^{\times}(n)\hookrightarrow\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}^{\times}_{n}(a)
. Furthermore, in both definitions of the maps
\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}^{\times}(n)\hookrightarrow\widetilde{\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)}
and
\operatorname{Gr}_{a}^{\times}(n)\hookrightarrow\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}^{\times}_{n}(a)
we have set
\phi_{i}(v_{i})=v_{i-1}
for all
i\neq 1
and
\phi_{1}(v_{1})=(-1)^{a-1}v_{n}
. Therefore the image in
\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}^{\times}_{n}(a)
from
\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}^{\times}(n)
by going down either side of the rhombus is the same.

As for the surjectivity of the map
\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}^{\times}(n)\twoheadrightarrow\operatorname{Conf}_{n}^{\times}(a)
, by Proposition
2.7
it suffices to show that any element of
\operatorname{Conf}_{n}^{\times}(a)
is the image of an element in
\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)
with
P=1
. But this is clear since we have
n
degrees of freedom to choose the isomorphisms
\phi_{i}
to put between the lines.
∎

2.4
Cluster structures

The pair
\big(\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}^{\times}(n),\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)\big)
admits a natural structure of cluster ensemble associated to Postnikov’s reduced plabic graphs
3
3

3

A rapid review on reduced plabic graphs has been included in the appendix. See also Postnikov’s article
[
26
]
for more details.
of rank
a
on a disk with
n
marked points on the boundary. In this section we focus on one particular reduced plabic graph
\Gamma_{a,n}
for each pair of parameters
(a,n)
, as depicted in the following picture:

\cdots
\vdots
\vdots
\vdots
\vdots
\vdots
\cdots
\cdots
\bullet
1
\bullet
2
\bullet
a-1
\bullet
a
\bullet
a+1
\bullet
a+2
\bullet
n-2
\bullet
n-1
\bullet
n

From a reduced plabic graph
\Gamma
we obtain a quiver
Q_{\Gamma}
by the following standard four-step procedure. This quiver (or its opposite) is used to study the cluster structure on Grassmannians by many others, including (but not restricted to) Postnikov
[
26
]
, Gekhtman, Shapiro, and Vainshtein
[
9
]
, and Rietsch and Williams
[
29
]
.

•

Assign a vertex to each face of
\Gamma
.

•

For each black vertex of
\Gamma
, draw a clockwise cycle of arrows as follows:

\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet

•

Remove a maximal subset of 2-cycles.

•

Freeze the vertices corresponding to boundary faces of
\Gamma
.

For example, the reduced plabic graph
\Gamma_{a,n}
gives rise to a quiver
Q_{a,n}
as follows (please keep in mind that we have the convention
n=a+b
). The unfrozen part of this quiver is also known as the triangle product
\mathrm{A}_{a-1}\boxtimes\mathrm{A}_{b-1}
(see, e.g.,
[
19
, p. 8]
,
[
35
, p. 2]
):

(0,0)
(1,1)
(1,2)
(1,3)
\cdots
(1,b-1)
(1,b)
(2,1)
(2,2)
(2,3)
\cdots
(2,b-1)
(2,b)
\vdots
\vdots
\vdots
\ddots
\vdots
\vdots
(a-1,1)
(a-1,2)
(a-1,3)
\cdots
(a-1,b-1)
(a-1,b)
(a,1)
(a,2)
(a,3)
\cdots
(a,b-1)
(a,b)

Here the vertex assigned to the top-left face is indexed by
(0,0)
. The other faces of
\Gamma_{a,n}
form an
a\times b
grid. Their corresponding vertices of
Q_{a,n}
are indexed in the same way as matrix entries. The gray vertices are frozen. Denote by
I
the set of vertices of
Q_{a,n}
and by
I^{\operatorname{uf}}
the set of unfrozen vertices of
Q_{a,n}
. The exchange matrix
\varepsilon
of
Q_{a,n}
is defined to be an
I\times I
matrix with entries

\varepsilon_{fg}=\#\{g\rightarrow f\}-\#\{f\rightarrow g\}.

Remark 2.10
.

The definition of the exchange matrix
\epsilon_{fg}
differs from the usual convention by an extra minus sign. The reason we include this extra minus sign is to simplify later computations.

For simplicity, we will also use an integer
i\in\{1,\dots,n\}
to denote the frozen vertex corresponding to the boundary face lying between
i
and
i+1
. In other words,

\text{frozen vertex $i$}=\begin{cases}(i,b)&\text{if $1\leq i\leq a$},\\
(a,n-i)&\text{if $a\leq i<n$},\\
(0,0)&\text{if $i=n$}.\end{cases}

Let
(\mathscr{A}_{a,n},\mathscr{X}_{a,n})
be the cluster ensemble associated to
Q_{a,n}
. See (
A.5
) for its rigorous definition. Let
\{A_{i,j}\}_{(i,j)\in I}
be the
K_{2}
cluster of
\mathscr{A}_{a,n}
associated to the quiver
Q_{a,n}
and let
\{X_{i,j}\}_{(i,j)\in I}
be the Poisson cluster of
\mathscr{X}_{a,n}
associated to
Q_{a,n}
.
Abusing notation, we will frequently write
f
instead of
(i,j)\in I
with
f
being the face of
\Gamma_{a,n}
corresponding to the vertex
(i,j)
of
Q_{a,n}
.

Cluster
\boldsymbol{K_{2}}
structure on
\boldsymbol{\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}^{\times}(n)}
.

We associate to each vertex
(i,j)
(including the vertex
(i,j)=(0,0)
) of
Q_{a,n}
an
a
-element set
4
4

4

The set is determined by the zig-zag strands of the reduced plabic graph
\Gamma_{a,n}
. See Appendix
B
for more details.

I(i,j):=\{\underbrace{b-j+1,\dots,b-j+i}_{\text{$i$ indices}},\underbrace{b+i+1,\dots,n}_{\text{$a-i$ indices}}\}.

(2.20)

Recall the Plücker coordinates
\Delta_{I}
of
\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}^{\times}(n)
. By defining

A_{i,j}:=\Delta_{I(i,j)},

(2.21)

we get a rational map

\psi\colon\ \mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}^{\times}(n)\dashrightarrow\mathscr{A}_{a,n}.

(2.22)

Scott
[
32
, Theorem 3]
showed that the pull-back map
\psi^{*}
gives an algebra isomorphism between
\mathcal{O}(\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}(n))
and the ordinary cluster algebra defined by the quiver
Q_{a,n}
; by allowing ourselves to invert the frozen variables we generalize her result to the following theorem.

Theorem 2.11
.

The pull-back map
\psi^{*}
is an algebra isomorphism between the upper cluster algebra
\mathbf{up}(\mathscr{A}_{a,n}):=\mathcal{O}(\mathscr{A}_{a,n})
and
\mathcal{O}\big(\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}^{\times}(n)\big)
.

Both
\mathcal{G}r_{a}^{\times}(n)
and
\mathscr{A}_{a,n}
are rational varieties. The map
\psi
induces an isomorphism between their function fields. Therefore the map
\psi\colon\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}^{\times}(n)\dashrightarrow\mathscr{A}_{a,n}
is birational.

Cluster Poisson structure on
\boldsymbol{\operatorname{Conf}_{n}^{\times}(a)}
.

Let
Q^{\operatorname{uf}}_{a,n}
denote the full subquiver of
Q_{a,n}
spanned by vertices in
I^{\operatorname{uf}}
. Let
\big(\mathscr{A}^{\operatorname{uf}}_{a,n},\mathscr{X}^{\operatorname{uf}}_{a,n}\big)
be the cluster ensemble associated to
Q^{\operatorname{uf}}_{a,n}
.

There is a canonical regular map
p\colon\mathscr{A}_{a,n}\rightarrow\mathscr{X}_{a,n}^{\operatorname{uf}}
defined on the cluster coordinate charts associated to
Q_{a,n}
such that

p^{*}(X_{g})=\prod_{f\in I}A_{f}^{\varepsilon_{fg}},\qquad\forall\,g\in I^{\operatorname{uf}}.

(2.23)

Recall the surjective map
\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}^{\times}(n)\rightarrow\operatorname{Conf}_{n}^{\times}(a)
in (
2.9
). We define a rational map

\psi\colon\ \operatorname{Conf}_{n}^{\times}(a)\dashrightarrow\mathscr{X}_{a,n}^{\operatorname{uf}}

(2.24)

by first taking a lift from
\operatorname{Conf}_{n}^{\times}(a)
to
\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}^{\times}(n)
, mapping over to
\mathscr{A}_{a,n}
via the birational equivalence (
2.22
), and then mapping it down to
\mathscr{X}_{a,n}^{\operatorname{uf}}
by the canonical
p
map. The map (
2.24
) is well-defined and does not depend on the lift, because
\psi^{*}(X_{g})
for
g\in I^{\operatorname{uf}}
is a ratio of Plücker coordintes with the same collection of indices (counted with multiplicity) in the numerator and in the denominator. It is known that (
2.24
) is a birational equivalence (see for example
[
35
]
).

Lemma 2.12
.

The restricted exchange matrix
\varepsilon|_{I\times I^{\operatorname{uf}}}
of
Q_{a,n}
is of full-rank.

Proof.

From (
2.23
) we see that the restriction of the map
p\colon\mathscr{A}_{a,n}\rightarrow\mathscr{X}_{a,n}^{\operatorname{uf}}
to a cluster coordinate chart is a map between algebraic tori defined by the matrix
\varepsilon|_{I\times I^{\operatorname{uf}}}
with integer entries. Therefore to show that
\varepsilon|_{I\times I^{\operatorname{uf}}}
is full-ranked, it suffices to show that
p\colon\mathscr{A}_{a,n}\rightarrow\mathscr{X}_{a,n}^{\operatorname{uf}}
is surjective.

By definition, we have the following commutative diagram

\textstyle{\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}^{\times}(n)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{\mathscr{A}_{a,n}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{\operatorname{Conf}_{n}^{\times}(a)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\textstyle{\mathscr{X}_{a,n}^{\operatorname{uf}}.}

with the map
p
on the left as defined in (
2.9
). Since both of the horizontal maps
\psi
are birational and the map
p
on the left is surjective, we know that the map
p
on the right is dominant. Note that the restriction of the map
p
on the right to each seed torus is a dominant morphism induced by a linear map between their character lattices, which forces it to be surjective. Therefore the map
p
on the right is surjective.
∎

Cluster
\boldsymbol{K_{2}}
structure on
\boldsymbol{\widetilde{\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)}}
.

The quiver
\widetilde{Q_{a,n}}
is obtained from
Q_{a,n}
by adding, for each frozen vertex
i
, a new frozen vertex
i^{\prime}
and a new arrow from
i
to
i^{\prime}
, so that the number of frozen vertices increases from
n
to
2n
. Denote the set of vertices of
\widetilde{Q_{a,n}}
by
\tilde{I}
and the exchange matrix of
\widetilde{Q_{a,n}}
by
\tilde{\varepsilon}
. For instance, the quiver
\widetilde{{Q}_{3,6}}
is as follows:

\square
6^{\prime}
\circ
\bullet
\bullet
\circ
\circ
\square
1^{\prime}
\bullet
\bullet
\circ
\circ
\square
2^{\prime}
\square
5^{\prime}
\square
4^{\prime}
\circ
\square
3^{\prime}

(2.25)

Let
\widetilde{\mathscr{A}_{a,n}}
be the cluster
K_{2}
variety associated to the quiver
\widetilde{Q_{a,n}}
.

Let
[\phi_{1},v_{1},\dots,\phi_{n},v_{n}]\in\widetilde{\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)}
. For each
1\leq i\leq n
we define a
scaling factor

\lambda_{i}
by

\phi(v_{i+1})=\begin{cases}\lambda_{i}v_{i}&\mbox{if }i\neq n,\\
(-1)^{a-1}\lambda_{n}v_{n}&\mbox{if }i=n.\end{cases}

(2.26)

We define a rational map

\tilde{\psi}\colon\ \widetilde{\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)}\dashrightarrow\widetilde{\mathscr{A}_{a,n}}

by setting
5
5

5

Throughout the paper we adopt the convention that the indices of
\lambda_{i}
are taken modulo
n
.

\tilde{\psi}^{*}\left(A_{f}\right):=\begin{cases}\Delta_{I(i,j)}&\mbox{if $f$ is a vertex $(i,j)$ of $Q_{a,n}$},\\
\lambda_{i-a}\displaystyle\frac{\Delta_{\{i-a,i-a+1,\dots,i-1\}}}{\Delta_{\{i-a+1,i-a+2,\dots,i\}}}&\mbox{if $f$ is a newly added vertex $i^{\prime}$}.\end{cases}

(2.27)

Corollary 2.13
.

The map
\tilde{\psi}\colon\widetilde{\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)}\dashrightarrow\widetilde{\mathscr{A}_{a,n}}
is birational.
Its pull-back map
\tilde{\psi}^{*}
is an algebra isomorphism between the upper cluster algebra
\mathbf{up}\big(\widetilde{\mathscr{A}_{a,n}}\big):=\mathcal{O}\big(\widetilde{\mathscr{A}_{a,n}}\big)
and
\mathcal{O}\big(\widetilde{\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)}\big)
.

Proof.

Let
{\rm H}=(\mathbb{G}_{m})^{n}
be the split algebraic torus with coordinates
(A_{1^{\prime}},\dots,A_{n^{\prime}})
. Note that there is no arrow between the vertices
i^{\prime}
and the unfrozen vertices in
Q_{a,n}
. Therefore the variables
A_{i^{\prime}}
do not affect the cluster mutations. Hence we get

\widetilde{\mathscr{A}_{a,n}}\cong\mathscr{A}_{a,n}\times{\rm H}.

On the other hand, Let
\mathrm{T}=(\mathbb{G}_{m}^{n})
be the split algebraic torus with coordinates
(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n})
. Note that the
\operatorname{SL}_{a}
-orbit of the
n
-tuple of vectors
(v_{1},\dots,v_{n})
satisfying the cyclic general position condition is captured by a point in
\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}^{\times}(n)
(Lemma
2.3
), and the
\operatorname{SL}_{a}
-orbit of the
n
-tuple of linear isomorphisms
(\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{n})
is captured by a point in
\mathrm{T}
. Hence we get

\widetilde{\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)}\cong\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}^{\times}(n)\times\mathrm{T}.

Now we have the following commutative diagram, where the vertical maps are a trivial
\mathrm{T}
-fiber bundle and a trivial
\mathrm{H}
-fiber bundle respectively

\begin{split}&\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern
