0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathscr{A}_{a,n}.}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces\end{split}

(2.28)

Recall from Theorem
2.11
that the bottom map
\psi
is a birational map which induces an isomorphism between algebras of regular functions. Moreover, since the Plücker coordinates in the second line of (
2.27
) are both invertible,
\tilde{\psi}
restricts to an isomorphism between the algebraic torus fibers over any pair of corresponding points on the bases. Therefore we can conclude that
\tilde{\psi}
is birational and
\tilde{\psi}^{*}
is an algebra isomorphism between
\mathbf{up}\big(\widetilde{\mathscr{A}_{a,n}}\big)
and
\mathcal{O}\big(\widetilde{\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)}\big)
.
∎

Cluster Poisson structure on
\boldsymbol{\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)}
.

Analogous to the map (
2.23
), there is a canonical map
p\colon\widetilde{\mathscr{A}_{a,n}}\rightarrow\mathscr{X}_{a,n}
defined on the cluster coordinate charts associated to
Q_{a,n}
by

p^{*}(X_{g}):=\prod_{f\in\tilde{I}}A_{f}^{\tilde{\varepsilon}_{fg}},\qquad\forall\,g\in I.

(2.29)

Let
[\phi_{1},l_{1},\dots,\phi_{n},l_{n}]\in{\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)}
. Let us lift it against
p\colon\widetilde{\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)}\twoheadrightarrow\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)
by picking a nonzero vector
v_{i}\in l_{i}
for each
i
. Composing with the map

p\circ\tilde{\psi}\colon\widetilde{\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)}{\dashrightarrow}\widetilde{\mathscr{A}_{a,n}}{\longrightarrow}\mathscr{X}_{a,n},

we get a rational map

\psi\colon\ \mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)\dashrightarrow\mathscr{X}_{a,n}.

The map
\psi
does not depend on the choices of
v_{i}\in l_{i}
. Indeed, if the vertex
(i,j)
is unfrozen, then
X_{i,j}
coincides with the cluster Poisson coordinates
X_{i,j}
on
\mathscr{X}_{a,n}^{\operatorname{uf}}
. For a frozen vertex
i
, one gets

\big(p\circ\tilde{\psi}\big)^{*}(X_{i})=\begin{cases}\lambda_{1-a}\dfrac{\Delta_{\{3-a,\dots,2\}}\Delta_{\{1-a,\dots,n\}}}{\Delta_{\{2-a,\dots,1\}}\Delta_{\{2,2-a,\dots,n\}}}&\text{if $i=1$},\\
\lambda_{i-a}\dfrac{\Delta_{\{i-a+2,\dots,i+1\}}\Delta_{\{2,\dots,i,i-a,\dots,n\}}}{\Delta_{\{i-a+1,\dots,i\}}\Delta_{\{2,\dots,i+1,i-a+1,\dots,n\}}}&\text{if $1<i<a$},\\
\lambda_{i-a}\dfrac{\Delta_{\{i-a,\dots,i-1\}}\Delta_{\{i-a+2,\dots,i,n\}}}{\Delta_{\{i-a+1,\dots,i\}}\Delta_{\{i-a+1,\dots,i-1,n\}}}&\text{if $a\leq i\leq n-1$},\\
\lambda_{b}\dfrac{\Delta_{\{b,b+2,\dots,n\}}}{\Delta_{\{b+1,\dots,n\}}}&\text{if $i=n$},\end{cases}

(2.30)

from which one can verify that
X_{i}
are independent of the choices of
v_{i}\in l_{i}
.

Proposition 2.14
.

The map
\psi\colon\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)\dashrightarrow\mathscr{X}_{a,n}
is a birational equivalence.

Proof.

Let
U
be an open subset of
\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)
consisting of
[\phi_{1},l_{1},\dots,\phi_{n},l_{n}]
such that

every collection
\{l_{i_{1}},\dots,l_{i_{a}}\}
of
a
-many lines is linearly independent.

(2.31)

Note that any Plücker coordinate on the lift of
U
to
\widetilde{\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}^{\times}_{n}(a)}\cong\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}^{\times}(n)\times\mathrm{H}
is nonzero.

Let
\mathcal{T}_{Q_{a,n}}\subset\mathscr{X}_{a,n}
be the algebraic torus corresponding to the cluster chart associated to
Q_{a,n}
. By definition, the map
\psi
restricted to
U
is a regular map to
\mathcal{T}_{Q_{a,n}}
.
Let
(X_{f})_{f\in I}
be a generic point in
\mathcal{T}_{Q_{a,n}}
. It suffices to show that it has a unique pre-image in
U
.

Recall that the map
\psi\colon\operatorname{Conf}_{n}^{\times}(a)\dashrightarrow\mathscr{X}_{a,n}^{\operatorname{uf}}
is birational. After imposing the genericity condition, by using the unfrozen part
(X_{f})_{f\in I^{\operatorname{uf}}}
, one can uniquely reconstruct a configuration of lines
[l_{1},\dots,l_{n}]
satisfying the condition (
2.31
). Take a representative
v_{i}\in l_{i}
for each
i
. One can use the frozen part
(X_{i})_{i=1}^{n}
to uniquely reconstruct the isomorphisms
\phi_{i}\colon l_{i}\rightarrow l_{i-1}
by deducing the scaling factors
\lambda_{i}
from the frozen variables
X_{i}
and the non-zero Plücker coordinates using (
2.30
). It is easy to see that the resulting isomorphisms
\phi_{i}
are independent of
v_{i}
chosen. Therefore we obtain a unique configuration in
U
.
∎

Theorem 2.15
.

The birational equivalence
\psi\colon\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)\dashrightarrow\mathscr{X}_{a,n}
induces an algebra isomorphism between
\mathbf{up}(\mathscr{X}_{a,n}):=\mathcal{O}(\mathscr{X}_{a,n})
and
\mathcal{O}\big(\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)\big)
. Combining this result with Proposition
2.6
we deduce that
\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}^{\times}_{n}(a)\cong\mathrm{Spec}(\mathbf{up}(\mathscr{X}_{a,n}))
.

Proof.

Note that we have the following commutative diagram

\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\tilde{\psi}}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{\widetilde{\mathscr{A}_{a,n}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\textstyle{\mathscr{X}_{a,n}.}

(2.32)

Since the map
\psi
is birational, its pull-back map
\psi^{*}
is an isomorphism between fields of rational functions on
\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)
and
\mathscr{X}_{a,n}
. Let
F
be a rational function on
\mathscr{X}_{a,n}
. It suffices to show that

\mbox{$F$ is regular on ${\mathscr{X}_{a,n}}$}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\mbox{$\psi^{*}(F)$ is regular on $\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)$.}

(2.33)

Let us use the other three maps in the commutative diagram (
2.32
) to prove the above statement. First by applying Lemma
A.1
to the vertical map
p
on the right, we know that

\mbox{$F$ is regular on ${\mathscr{X}_{a,n}}$}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\mbox{$p^{*}(F)$ is regular on $\widetilde{\mathscr{A}_{a,n}}$.}

On the other hand, note that the vertical map
p
on the left is a surjective morphism onto a smooth affine variety. By applying Lemma
2.16
below we can deduce that

\mbox{$\varphi^{*}(F)$ is regular on $\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)$}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\mbox{$p^{*}\circ\psi^{*}(F)$ is regular on $\widetilde{\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)}$.}

By the commutativity of diagram (
2.32
), we know that
p^{*}\circ\psi^{*}(F)=\tilde{\psi}^{*}\circ p^{*}(F)
. Therefore the claim (
2.33
) reduces to

\mbox{$p^{*}(F)$ is regular on $\widetilde{\mathscr{A}_{a,n}}$}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\mbox{$\tilde{\psi}^{*}\circ p^{*}(F)$ is regular on $\widetilde{\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)}$,}

which is true by Corollary
2.13
.
∎

Lemma 2.16
.

Let
f\colon\operatorname{Spec}B\rightarrow\operatorname{Spec}A
be a surjective morphism where
A
is a normal Noetherian domain. Let
\operatorname{Fr}(A)
be the field of fractions of
A
. Then for any
F\in\operatorname{Fr}(A)
, we have
F\in A
if and only if
f^{*}(F)\in B
.

Proof.

The implication
F\in A\implies f^{*}(F)\in B
is trivial.

Conversely, suppose
f^{*}(F)\in B
but
F\notin A
. Since
A
is a normal Noetherian domain, a standard result of commutative algebra (e.g.,
[
4
, Corollary 11.4]
) states that

A=\bigcap_{\operatorname{codim}\mathfrak{p}=1}A_{\mathfrak{p}}.

If
F\notin A
, then there exists a prime ideal
\mathfrak{p}
of codimension 1 such that
F\notin A_{\mathfrak{p}}
. Since
A_{\mathfrak{p}}
is a discrete valuation ring,
F\notin A_{\mathfrak{p}}
implies that
\frac{1}{F}\in\mathfrak{p}A_{\mathfrak{p}}
. Let
\mathfrak{q}\in{\rm Spec}~B
be a preimage of
\mathfrak{p}
under the morphism
f
. Here
\mathfrak{q}
is a prime ideal in
B
, and
f^{\ast}
is local homomorphism from
A_{\mathfrak{p}}
to
B_{\mathfrak{q}}
. Hence,
f^{*}\big(\frac{1}{F}\big)\in\mathfrak{q}B_{\mathfrak{q}}
. Combining this with the assumption that
f^{*}(F)\in B
we deduce that

1=f^{*}(F)f^{*}\left(\frac{1}{F}\right)\in\mathfrak{q}B_{\mathfrak{q}},

which is absurd. Therefore
F
must be an element of
A
.
∎

Let us end the section with a disclaimer of notation convention for the rest of the paper.

Convention 2.17
.

Let us summarize the following algebra isomorphisms:

\displaystyle\text{Theorem \ref{Scott}}\colon\ \mathcal{O}\big(\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}^{\times}(n)\big)\cong\mathbf{up}\left(\mathscr{A}_{a,n}\right),

\displaystyle\text{Corollary \ref{A isomorphism}}\colon\ \mathcal{O}\big(\widetilde{\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)}\big)\cong\mathbf{up}\big(\widetilde{\mathscr{A}_{a,n}}\big),

\displaystyle\text{Theorem \ref{X isomorphism}}\colon\ \mathcal{O}\big(\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)\big)\cong\mathbf{up}(\mathscr{X}_{a,n}).

From now on, we will treat each pair as
identical algebras
, and treat their corresponding fields of rational functions as
identical fields
as well. In other words, we will drop the pull-back maps
\psi^{*}
and
\tilde{\psi}^{*}
to simplify our notations. We view
\{A_{f}\}
as regular functions on
\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}^{\times}(n)
and view
\{X_{f}\}
as rational functions on
\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)
. In particular, using the cluster Poisson coordinates
\{X_{f}\}
we also get a log-canonical Poisson structure (see
[
5
]
for more details) on
\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)
where

\{X_{f},X_{g}\}=2\varepsilon_{fg}X_{f}X_{g}.

(2.34)

Recall from the proof of Corollary
2.13
that
\widetilde{\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)}\cong\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}^{\times}(n)\times\mathrm{H}
. We can pull back any Plücker coordinate
\Delta_{I}
on
\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}^{\times}(n)
to a regular function on
\widetilde{\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)}
, which we will also denote by
\Delta_{I}
as well.

3
Cluster nature of decorated configuration space

This section is devoted to studying the cluster nature of the following three maps on
\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)
: the twisted monodromy
P
, the cyclic rotation
R
, and the potential function
\mathcal{W}
, with the goal to express them in terms of the cluster coordinates
\{X_{f}\}_{f\in I}
associated to the quiver
Q_{a,n}
.

3.1
Twisted monodromy and Casimir

Proposition 3.1
.

The twisted monodromy

P=\prod_{f\in I}X_{f}.

The function
P
is a
Casimir element
with respect to the Poisson bracket (
2.34
), that is,
\{P,F\}\allowbreak=0
for any rational function
F
on
\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)
.

Proof.

Let
[\phi_{1},l_{1},\dots,\phi_{n},l_{n}]\in\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)
.
Let us lift it against the projection
p\colon\widetilde{\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)}\twoheadrightarrow\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)
to a point
[\phi_{1},v_{1},\dots,\phi_{n},v_{n}]
. By (
2.29
), we get

p^{*}\left(\prod_{f\in I}X_{f}\right)=\prod_{(f,g)\in I\times\tilde{I}}A_{g}^{\tilde{\varepsilon}_{gf}}=\prod_{g\in\tilde{I}}A_{g}^{\sum\limits_{f\in I}\tilde{\varepsilon}_{gf}}.

(3.1)

Note that the quiver
Q_{a,n}
is made of cycles. Therefore for every
f\in I
, one has

\sum_{g\in I}\varepsilon_{gf}=0.

(3.2)

Hence the only factors contributing to the product (
3.1
) are from the extra frozen vertices that extend
Q_{a,n}
to
\widetilde{Q_{a,n}}
. Therefore

p^{*}\left(\prod_{f\in I}X_{f}\right)=\prod_{i=1}^{n}A_{i^{\prime}}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{\lambda_{i-a}\Delta_{\{i-a,i-a+1,\dots,i-1\}}}{\Delta_{\{i-a+1,i-a+2,\dots,i\}}}=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}.

By comparing (
2.26
) with the definition of
P
in (
2.5
), we see that
\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}=p^{*}(P)
. But
p^{*}\Big(\prod\limits_{f\in I}X_{f}\Big)=p^{*}(P)
implies
\prod\limits_{f\in I}X_{f}=P
automatically since
p
is a projection.

The statement that
P
is Casimir is a direct consequence of (
3.2
) and (
2.34
).
∎

3.2
Cyclic rotation and cluster transformation

Recall the twisted cyclic rotation
C_{a}
on
\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}^{\times}(n)
.
Gekhtman, Shapiro, and Vainshtein proved that
C_{a}
is a cluster
K_{2}
automorphism that can be realized by a mutation sequence
\rho
(see
[
9
, p. 90]
). In this section, we briefly recall the definition of
\rho
.
We show that the cyclic rotation
R
on
\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)
is a cluster Poisson automorphism realized by the same mutation sequence
\rho
.

The vertices of the quiver
Q_{a,n}
are indexed by
(i,j)\in I
. Let
\rho
be a mutation sequence hitting in the unfrozen part of
Q_{a,n}
along every column from bottom to top, starting at the leftmost unfrozen column and going all the way to the rightmost unfrozen column, that is,

\rho:=\sigma_{b-1}\circ\cdots\circ\sigma_{2}\circ\sigma_{1},\qquad\mbox{where}\quad\sigma_{i}:=\mu_{(1,i)}\circ\mu_{(2,i)}\circ\dots\circ\mu_{(a-1,i)}.

(3.3)

Equivalently, the sequence
\rho
may be realized by a sequence of 2-by-2 moves
6
6

6

An explicit realization of
\rho
as a sequence of 2-by-2 moves has been included in the appendix.
on the reduced plabic graph
\Gamma_{a,n}
. Its resulting reduced plabic graph
\Gamma_{a,n}^{\prime}
is identical to
\Gamma_{a,n}
with the non-boundary faces remaining in the same places and with all the boundary faces rotated to the neighboring one in the clockwise direction.

\cdots
\vdots
\vdots
\vdots
\vdots
\vdots
\cdots
\cdots
\bullet
1
\bullet
2
\bullet
a-1
\bullet
a
\bullet
a+1
\bullet
a+2
\bullet
n-2
\bullet
n-1
\bullet
n
\Gamma_{a,n}
\cdots
\vdots
\vdots
\vdots
\vdots
\vdots
\cdots
\cdots
\bullet
n
\bullet
1
\bullet
a-2
\bullet
a-1
\bullet
a
\bullet
a+1
\bullet
n-3
\bullet
n-2
\bullet
n-1
\Gamma^{\prime}_{a,n}

One advantage of using reduced plabic graphs is that one may make use of zig-zag strands to assign an
a
-element subset of
\{1,\dots,n\}
to each face of the graph (see Definition
B.2
). For example, the
a
-element subsets assigned to faces of
\Gamma_{a,n}
in (
2.20
) arise precisely in this way, which in turn associates Plücker coordinates to these faces. Moreover, it is not hard to see that a 2-by-2 move combined with the cluster
K_{2}
mutation formula yields precisely a Plücker relation (see (
B.1
) for more details); hence we can conclude that the Plücker coordinates on any bipartite graph obtained from
\Gamma_{a,n}
via a sequence of 2-by-2 moves can be computed by using zig-zag strands as well.

Let
\{A^{\prime}_{f}\}
be the
K_{2}
cluster associated to
\Gamma^{\prime}_{a,n}
after applying the mutation sequence
\rho
.
According to the above discussion, the cluster
\{A^{\prime}_{f}\}
is defined by the
a
-element sets (
2.20
) assigned to faces of the minimal bipartitie graphs
\Gamma^{\prime}_{a,n}
. Using the zig-zag strands on
\Gamma^{\prime}_{a,n}
, we find that

I^{\prime}(i,j)=\{\underbrace{b-j,\dots,b-j+i-1}_{\text{consecutive $i$ indices}},\underbrace{b+i,\dots,n-1}_{\text{consecutive $a-i$ indices}}\},

(3.4)

which yields

A^{\prime}_{i,j}=\Delta_{I^{\prime}(i,j)}=C_{a}^{*}(A_{i,j}).

In other words, the twisted cyclic rotation
C_{a}
is equal to the cluster transformation defined by the mutation sequence
\rho
.

If we apply the mutation sequence
\rho
to the extended quiver
\widetilde{{Q}_{a,n}}
, it is easy to see that the resulting quiver
\rho\widetilde{Q_{a,n}}
is again the same as
\widetilde{{Q}_{a,n}}
up to rotations of frozen vertices. For example, if we start with
\widetilde{Q_{3,6}}
as in (
2.25
), then
\rho\widetilde{Q_{3,6}}
is as follows:

\square
6^{\prime}
\circ
5
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\bullet
\square
1^{\prime}
\square
2^{\prime}
\circ
6
\circ
1
\circ
4
\circ
3
\square
5^{\prime}
\square
4^{\prime}
\circ
2
\square
3^{\prime}

In particular, since the extra frozen vertex
i^{\prime}
is only connected to the frozen vertex
i
, any cluster mutation does not change such connectivity. Therefore after the mutation sequence
\rho
, the frozen vertex
i^{\prime}
remains connected to
i
. Recall from the commutative diagram (
2.28
) that
\widetilde{\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)}
fibers over
\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)
the same way
\widetilde{\mathscr{A}_{a,n}}
fibers over
\mathscr{A}_{a,n}
, with the data of the linear isomorphisms
\phi_{i}
captured by the extra frozen cluster
K_{2}
coordinates
A_{i^{\prime}}
. By an additional checking on the pull-backs of the frozen cluster
K_{2}
coordiantes
A_{i^{\prime}}
we see that the twisted cyclic rotation
\tilde{C}_{a}\colon[\phi_{1},v_{1},\dots,\phi_{n},v_{n}]\mapsto\big[\phi_{n},(-1)^{a-1}v_{n},\phi_{1},v_{1},\dots,\phi_{n-1},v_{n-1}\big]
on
\widetilde{\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)}
is equal to the cluster transformation defined by the mutation sequence
\rho
. Pushing the twisted cyclic rotation
\tilde{C}_{a}
down via the projection
\widetilde{\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)}\twoheadrightarrow\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)
and using the commutative diagram (
2.32
), we see that the cyclic rotation
R
on
\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)
is also equal to the cluster transformation defined by the mutation sequence
\rho
with

X^{\prime}_{g}=R^{*}(X_{g}).

In particular, since the cyclic rotation
R
is a cluster transformation, it preserves the Poisson structure on
\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)
.

3.3
Potential function

Proposition 3.2
.

In terms of the Poisson cluster
\{X_{i,j}\}
associated to
Q_{a,n}
, the theta functions
\vartheta_{i}
in (
2.8
) are

\displaystyle\vartheta_{n}=X_{0,0},\qquad\vartheta_{a}=X_{a,b},

(3.5)

\displaystyle\vartheta_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{b}X_{i,b}X_{i,b-1}\cdots X_{i,j}\qquad\text{for $0<i<a$},

(3.6)

\displaystyle\vartheta_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{a}X_{a,n-i}X_{a-1,n-i}\cdots X_{j,n-i}\qquad\text{for $a<i<n$},

(3.7)

Remark 3.3
.

For (
3.6
), the terms in
\vartheta_{i}
are in bijection with rectangles of all possible lengths across the
i
th row of the quiver
Q_{a,n}
that ends at the vertex
(i,b)
.
For (
3.7
), the terms in
\vartheta_{i}
are in bijection with rectangles of all possible heights across the
(n-i)
th column of
Q_{a_{n}}
that rises from the vertex
(a,n-i)
. For instance, the formulas of
\vartheta_{2}
and
\vartheta_{5}
with
a=3
and
n=7
are as follows:

(1,1)
(2,1)
(1,2)
(2,2)
(1,3)
(2,3)
(1,4)
(2,4)
(3,1)
(3,2)
(3,3)
(3,4)
(0,0)

\displaystyle\vartheta_{2}=X_{2,4}+X_{2,3}X_{2,4}+X_{2,2}X_{2,3}X_{2,4}+X_{2,1}X_{2,2}X_{2,3}X_{2,4},

\displaystyle\vartheta_{5}=\vartheta_{7-2}=X_{3,2}+X_{2,2}X_{3,2}+X_{1,2}X_{2,2}X_{3,2}.

Therefore the
potential function

\mathcal{W}
in (
2.9
) can be expressed as

\mathcal{W}=X_{0,0}+X_{a,b}+\sum_{i=1}^{a-1}\sum_{j=1}^{b}X_{i,b}X_{i,b-1}\cdots X_{i,j}+\sum_{j=1}^{b-1}\sum_{i=1}^{a}X_{a,j}X_{a-1,j}\cdots X_{i,j}.

(3.8)

Proof.

We again lift the computation up to
\widetilde{\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)}
. By the definition of
\vartheta_{n}
we have
\phi(v_{b+1})-p^{*}(\vartheta_{n})v_{b+1}\in{\rm Span}(v_{b+2},\dots,v_{n})
. Hence

\displaystyle 0=\Delta_{(\phi(v_{b+1})-p^{*}(\vartheta_{n})v_{b+1})\wedge v_{b+2}\wedge\dots\wedge v_{n}}=\lambda_{b}\Delta_{\{b,b+2,\dots,n\}}-p^{*}(\vartheta_{n})\Delta_{\{b+1,\dots,n\}}.

Therefore

p^{*}(\vartheta_{n})=\lambda_{b}\frac{\Delta_{\{b,b+2,\dots,n\}}}{\Delta_{\{b+1,\dots,n\}}}=p^{*}(X_{0,0}),

(3.9)

where the second equality follows from (
2.30
).
The above equality then implies
\vartheta_{n}=X_{0,0}
due to the surjectivity of
p
. Similarly we prove that
\vartheta_{a}=X_{a,b}
.

Now let us prove (
3.7
). The proof of (
3.6
) goes along the same line.

For
a<i<n
, let
k=n-i
. Then
0<k<b=n-a
. For
1\leq j\leq a
, define the
a
-element set

J(j,k):=\{b-k,\underbrace{b-k+2,\dots,b-k+j}_{\text{consecutive $j-1$ indices}},\underbrace{b+j+1,\dots,n}_{\text{consecutive $a-j$ indices}}\}.

Recall the definition of
a
-element sets
I(j,k)
from (
2.20
).
One has the Plücker relation (
2.3
):

\Delta_{I(j,k-1)}\Delta_{I(j+1,k+1)}+\Delta_{I(j+1,k)}\Delta_{J(j,k)}=\Delta_{I(j,k)}\Delta_{J(j+1,k)}.

Dividing by
\Delta_{I(j,k-1)}\Delta_{I(j+1,k+1)}
on both sides, we get

1+\frac{\Delta_{I(j+1,k)}\Delta_{J(j,k)}}{\Delta_{I(j,k-1)}\Delta_{I(j+1,k+1)}}=\frac{\Delta_{I(j,k)}\Delta_{J(j+1,k)}}{\Delta_{I(j,k-1)}\Delta_{I(j+1,k+1)}}.

Let us set

Y_{j,k}:=1+X_{j,k}(1+X_{j-1,k}(\dots(1+X_{1,k})\dots)).

Let us fix
k
. We will prove by induction on
j
that

p^{*}(Y_{j,k})=\frac{\Delta_{I(j,k)}\Delta_{J(j+1,k)}}{\Delta_{I(j,k-1)}\Delta_{I(j+1,k+1)}}.

(3.10)

Note that
I(1,k+1)=J(1,k)
. Therefore for
j=1
, by using (
2.29
) for
p^{*}
and identifying
A_{f}=\Delta_{I(f)}
we have

\displaystyle p^{*}(Y_{1,k})=1+p^{*}(X_{1,k})=1+\frac{\Delta_{I(1,k+1)}\Delta_{I(2,k)}}{\Delta_{I(1,k-1)}\Delta_{I(2,k+1)}}=\frac{\Delta_{I(1,k)}\Delta_{J(2,k)}}{\Delta_{I(1,k-1)}\Delta_{I(2,k+1)}}.

If
1<j<a
and
(
3.10
)
is true for
j-1
, then it is true for
j
because

p^{*}(Y_{j,k})=1+p^{*}(X_{j,k}Y_{j-1,k})=1+\frac{\Delta_{I(j+1,k)}\Delta_{J(j,k)}}{\Delta_{I(j,k-1)}\Delta_{I(j+1,k+1)}}=\frac{\Delta_{I(j,k)}\Delta_{J(j+1,k)}}{\Delta_{I(j,k-1)}\Delta_{I(j+1,k+1)}},

and the induction is finished.

Recall from (
2.30
) that

p^{*}(X_{a,k})=p^{*}(X_{n-k})=\lambda_{b-k}\frac{\Delta_{\{b-k,\dots,n-k-1\}}\Delta_{\{b-k+2,\dots,n-k,n\}}}{\Delta_{\{b-k+1,\dots,n-k\}}\Delta_{\{b-k+1,\dots,n-k-1,n\}}}.

Now by setting
j=a-1
in (
3.10
) and recalling (
2.20
) for the definition of
I(j,k)
, we get

\displaystyle p^{*}(X_{a,k}Y_{a-1,k})=p^{*}(X_{a,k})p^{*}(Y_{a-1,k})

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\lambda_{b-k}\frac{\Delta_{\{b-k,\dots,n-k-1\}}\Delta_{\{b-k+2,\dots,n-k,n\}}}{\Delta_{\{b-k+1,\dots,n-k\}}\Delta_{\{b-k+1,\dots,n-k-1,n\}}}\frac{\Delta_{\{b-k+1,\dots,n-k-1,n\}}\Delta_{\{b-k,b-k+2,\dots,n-k\}}}{\Delta_{\{b-k+2,\dots,n-k,n\}}\Delta_{\{b-k,\dots,n-k-1\}}}

\displaystyle\qquad{}=\lambda_{b-k}\frac{\Delta_{\{b-k,b-k+2,\dots,n-k\}}}{\Delta_{\{b-k+1,b-k+2,\dots,n-k\}}}=p^{*}(\vartheta_{n-k}),

where the last equality is obtained analogously to (
3.9
). This shows that

\vartheta_{n-k}=X_{a,k}Y_{a-1,k}=\sum_{j=1}^{a}X_{a,k}X_{a-1,k}\cdots X_{j,k}.

3.4
Tropicalization

Cluster varieties are examples of
positive spaces
, which admit canonical tropicalizations. The sets of integral tropical points of cluster varieties play important roles in the Fock–Goncharov cluster duality. We briefly review the related constructions in this subsection; please see
[
5
]
for more details.

Let
\mathscr{X}
be a cluster variety (either
K_{2}
type or Poisson type). A
positive rational function
on
\mathscr{X}
is a nonzero function that can be expressed as a ratio of two polynomials with non-negative integer coefficients in one (and hence all) cluster(s) of
\mathscr{X}
. The set
{P}(\mathscr{X})
of positive rational functions is a
semifield
, i.e., a set closed under addition, multiplication, and division. The pair
(\mathscr{X},P(\mathscr{X}))
is called a positive space.

Let
\mathbb{Z}^{t}:=\left(\mathbb{Z},\min,+\right)
be the semifield of tropical integers.
7
7

7

There is another closely-related semifield
\mathbb{Z}^{T}:=(\mathbb{Z},\max,+)
. See
[
17
, p. 35]
for its connection to
\mathbb{Z}^{t}
.

We define the tropicalization of
(\mathscr{X},P(\mathscr{X}))
to be the set

\mathscr{X}\big(\mathbb{Z}^{t}\big):=\operatorname{Hom}_{\text{semifield}}\big(P(\mathscr{X}),\mathbb{Z}^{t}\big).

Let
(\mathscr{X},P(\mathscr{X}))
and
(\mathscr{Y},P(\mathscr{Y}))
be a pair of cluster varieties. A rational map
f\colon\mathscr{X}\dashrightarrow\mathscr{Y}
is
positive
if the pull back
f^{*}P(\mathscr{Y})
is contained in
P(\mathscr{X})
. Every positive rational map
f
admits a tropicalization
f^{t}\colon\mathscr{X}(\mathbb{Z}^{t})\rightarrow\mathscr{Y}\big(\mathbb{Z}^{t}\big)
defined by precomposing with the pull-back map
f^{*}
. In particular cluster mutations are subtraction-free. Therefore every cluster automorphism is positive and can be tropicalized. It induces a natural action of the cluster modular group
\mathcal{G}
on
\mathscr{X}\big(\mathbb{Z}^{t}\big)
.

Let
\chi:=\left\{X_{i}\,|\,1\leq i\leq m\right\}
be a cluster coordinate chart of
\mathscr{X}
. Its tropicalization is a bijection

\chi^{t}\colon\ \mathscr{X}\big(\mathbb{Z}^{t}\big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathbb{Z}^{m},\qquad l\longmapsto\left(l(X_{1}),l(X_{2}),\dots,l(X_{m})\right):=(x_{1},\dots,x_{m}).

The
m
-tuple
(x_{1},\dots,x_{m})
is called the
tropical coordinates
on
\mathscr{X}\big(\mathbb{Z}^{t}\big)
in terms of the cluster
\chi
.

Tautologically, every positive function
f\in P(\mathscr{X})
gives rise to a
\mathbb{Z}
-valued function

f^{t}\colon\ \mathscr{X}\big(\mathbb{Z}^{t}\big)\longrightarrow\mathbb{Z},\qquad l\longmapsto l(f),

which can be expressed as a piecewise linear function in terms of the tropical coordinates
\left(x_{1},\dots,x_{m}\right)
by the following procedure:

1.

Change every addition to taking minimum.

2.

Change every multiplication into addition and every division to subtraction.

3.

Drop the coefficient of each monomial in the function.

4.

Change the variables
X_{i}
to
x_{i}
.

For example, the tropicalization of the positive rational function
f=\frac{2X_{1}X_{2}+X_{2}^{2}+1}{(X_{1}+X_{2})^{3}}
is

f^{t}=\min\{x_{1}+x_{2},2x_{2},0\}-3\min\{x_{1},x_{2}\}.

Let us tropicalize the cluster variety
\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}^{\times}_{n}(a)
. Recall that the potential
\mathcal{W}
and the twisted monodromy
P
are positive functions on
\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}^{\times}_{n}(a)
.
We can hence define subsets

Q(a,b,c):=\left\{q\in\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}^{\times}_{n}(a)\left(\mathbb{Z}^{t}\right)\,\Big|\,\begin{matrix}\mathcal{W}^{t}(q)\geq 0,\\
P^{t}(q)=c\end{matrix}\right\}.

(3.11)

Remark 3.4
.

The part of
\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)\big(\mathbb{Z}^{t}\big)
that is cut out by the inequality
\mathcal{W}^{t}\geq 0
is also known as the
superpotential cone
, and as we will see soon, it coincides with the
Gelfand–Zetlin cone
as well.

The rotation
R\colon\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}^{\times}_{n}(a)\rightarrow\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}^{\times}_{n}(a)
is a cluster transformation and therefore can be tropicalized into
R^{t}\colon\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}^{\times}_{n}(a)\big(\mathbb{Z}^{t}\big)\rightarrow\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}^{\times}_{n}(a)\big(\mathbb{Z}^{t}\big)
. By definition
\mathcal{W}
and
P
are invariant under
R
, and hence
R^{t}
preserves the sets
Q(a,b,c)
. We will introduce another coordinate system on
\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}^{\times}_{n}(a)\big(\mathbb{Z}^{t}\big)
in the next section and construct a natural bijection between
Q(a,b,c)
and the set of plane partitions
P(a,b,c)
defined in Definition
1.5
.

3.5
Gelfand–Zetlin coordinates

Although we can express the potential function
\mathcal{W}
in terms of the cluster Poisson coordinates
X_{f}
in a subtraction-free manner, the resulting tropicalization does not convey its nice combinatorial connection to plane partitions very well. In this section, we will do a monomial transformation on the cluster Poisson coordinates to define a new set of coordinates, which we call “Gelfand–Zetlin coordinates” due to its combinatorial connection to Gelfand–Zetlin patterns, a well-studied object in combinatorial representation theory.

Definition 3.5
.

We define the
Gelfand–Zetlin coordinate

L_{i,j}
associated to the vertex
(i,j)
of the quiver
Q_{a,n}
to be

L_{i,j}:=\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\geq i\\
l\geq j\end{subarray}}X_{k,l}.

By definition these new functions
\{L_{i,j}\}
relate to the cluster Poisson coordinates
\{X_{i,j}\}
by a monomial transformation. With the convention that
L_{i,j}=1
for
i>a
or
j>b
, we can express the inverse of this transformation as

X_{0,0}=\frac{L_{0,0}}{L_{1,1}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad X_{i,j}=\frac{L_{i,j}L_{i+1,j+1}}{L_{i+1,j}L_{i,j+1}}\qquad\text{for $(i,j)\neq(0,0)$}.

It follows that these new functions
L_{i,j}
form a coordinate system on
\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)
, which justifies the term “coordinate” in the definition.

The following Lemma is a direct consequence of Proposition
3.1
and (
3.8
).

Lemma 3.6
.

In terms of the Gelfand–Zetlin coordinates, the twisted monodromy

P=L_{0,0}
, and the potential

\mathcal{W}=\frac{L_{0,0}}{L_{1,1}}+L_{a,b}+\sum_{i=1}^{a-1}\sum_{j=1}^{b}\frac{L_{i,j}}{L_{i+1,j}}+\sum_{j=1}^{b-1}\sum_{i=1}^{a}\frac{L_{i,j}}{L_{i,j+1}}.

(3.12)

Remark 3.7
.

By setting
L_{0,0}={\rm e}^{t}
, (
3.12
) coincides with the Lax operator on Grassmannian in
[
2
, equation (B.25)]
.

The Gelfand–Zetlin coordinates
L_{i,j}
are clearly positive functions on
\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)
. Let us follow our convention and use lower case letter
l_{i,j}
to denote the tropicalization of
L_{i,j}
. The next proposition shows that plane partitions can be obtained naturally via tropicalization of the Gelfand–Zetlin coordinates.

Proposition 3.8
.

The tropicalized change of coordinates
(x_{i,j})\mapsto(l_{i,j})
gives a natural bijection between the subset
Q(a,b,c)\subset\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}^{\times}_{a}(n)\big(\mathbb{Z}^{t}\big)
and the set of plane partitions
P(a,b,c)
defined in Definition
1.5
.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.6
, we get

\displaystyle P^{t}=l_{0,0},

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}^{t}=\min\left(\{l_{0,0}-l_{1,1},l_{a,b}\}\cup\left(\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq a-1\\
1\leq j\leq b\end{subarray}}\{l_{i,j}-l_{i+1,j}\}\right)\cup\left(\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq a\\
1\leq j\leq b-1\end{subarray}}\{l_{i,j}-l_{i,j+1}\}\right)\right).

The condition that
\mathcal{W}^{t}\geq 0
is now equivalent to the conditions

l_{0,0}\geq l_{1,1},\qquad l_{a,b}\geq 0,\qquad l_{i,j-1}\geq l_{i,j}\geq l_{i+1,j},\qquad\forall\,(i,j).

(3.13)

After imposing the additional condition that
P^{t}=l_{0,0}=c
, the coordinates
(l_{i,j})_{\begin{subarray}{c}1\leq i\leq a\\
1\leq j\leq b\end{subarray}}
of the elements of
Q(a,b,c)
(
3.11
) naturally become plane partitions in
P(a,b,c)
.
∎

Remark 3.9
.

Note the similarity between the inequalities (
3.13
) and the inequalities in Gelfand–Zetlin patterns. For this reason, we name the coordinates
L_{ij}
as “Gelfand–Zetlin” coordinates.

The next lemma is useful for future computation with Gelfand–Zetlin coordinates.

Lemma 3.10
.

Recall
\lambda_{k}
in (
2.26
) and
A_{i,j}
in (
2.21
). Set
A_{i,0}=A_{0,j}=A_{0,0}
.
Then

p^{*}\left(L_{i,j}\right)=\frac{A_{i-1,j-1}}{A_{i,j}}\prod_{k=i-a}^{b-j}\lambda_{k},\qquad\forall\,1\leq i\leq a,\qquad\forall\,1\leq j\leq b,

(3.14)

where the indices of
\lambda_{k}
are defined modulo
n
and run as
i-a,i-a+1,\dots,b-j
.

Proof.

We prove (
3.14
) by a double induction on the indices
(i,j)
in the descending order.
It is certainly true for
(a,b)
since

p^{*}(L_{a,b})=p^{*}(X_{a,b})=\lambda_{n}\frac{\Delta_{\{2,\dots,a,n\}}}{\Delta_{\{1,2,\dots,a\}}}=\lambda_{n}\frac{A_{a-1,b-1}}{A_{a,b}}.

Take
(a,j)
with
1\leq j<b
. The cluster coordinate
X_{a,j}
is associated to a boundary face. By (
2.30
) and (
2.20
),

p^{*}(X_{a,j})=p^{*}(X_{n-j})=\lambda_{b-j}\frac{\Delta_{\{b-j,b-j+1,\dots,n-j-1\}}\Delta_{\{b-j+2,\dots,n-j,n\}}}{\Delta_{\{b-j+1,b-j+2,\dots,n-j\}}\Delta_{\{b-j+1,\dots,n-j-1,n\}}}=\lambda_{b-j}\frac{A_{a,j+1}}{A_{a,j}}\frac{A_{a-1,j-1}}{A_{a-1,j}}.

If (
3.14
) is true for
(a,j+1)
, then

\displaystyle p^{*}(L_{a,j})=p^{*}(X_{a,j}L_{a,j+1})

\displaystyle=\left(\lambda_{b-j}\frac{A_{a,j+1}A_{a-1,j-1}}{A_{a,j}A_{a-1,j}}\right)\left(\frac{A_{a-1,j}}{A_{a,j+1}}\prod_{k=i-a}^{b-j-1}\lambda_{k}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{A_{a-1,j-1}}{A_{a,j}}\prod_{k=i-a}^{b-j}\lambda_{k}.

Similarly, for
(i,b)
with
1\leq i<a
, by induction we get

\displaystyle p^{*}(L_{i,b})=p^{*}(X_{i,b}L_{i+1,b})

\displaystyle=\left(\lambda_{i-a}\frac{A_{i+1,b}A_{i-1,b-1}}{A_{i,b}A_{i,b-1}}\right)\left(\frac{A_{i,b-1}}{A_{i+1,b}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}k=\\
i-a+1\end{subarray}}^{n}\lambda_{k}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{A_{i-1,b-1}}{A_{i,b}}\prod_{k=i-a}^{n}\lambda_{k}.

Take
(i,j)
with
i<a
and
j<b
. Recall from (
2.29
) that

p^{*}(X_{i,j})=\frac{A_{i-1,j-1}A_{i,j+1}A_{i+1,j}}{A_{i-1,j}A_{i,j-1}A_{i+1,j+1}}.

If (
3.14
) is true for
(i+1,j)
,
(i,j+1)
and
(i+1,j+1)
, then

\displaystyle p^{*}(L_{i,j})=p^{*}\left(\frac{X_{i,j}L_{i+1,j}L_{i,j+1}}{L_{i+1,j+1}}\right)=\left(\frac{A_{i-1,j-1}A_{i,j+1}A_{i+1,j}}{A_{i-1,j}A_{i,j-1}A_{i+1,j+1}}\right)\left(\frac{A_{i,j-1}}{A_{i+1,j}}\prod_{k=i-a+1}^{b-j}\lambda_{k}\right)

\displaystyle\hphantom{p^* (L_{i,j} ) =}{}\times\left(\frac{A_{i-1,j}}{A_{i,j+1}}\prod_{k=i-a}^{b-j-1}\lambda_{k}\right)\left(\frac{A_{i,j}}{A_{i+1,j+1}}\prod_{k=i-a+1}^{b-j-1}\lambda_{k}\right)^{-1}=\frac{A_{i-1,j-1}}{A_{i,j}}\prod_{k=i-a}^{b-j}\lambda_{k}.

∎

4
Cluster duality

4.1
Duality conjecture and canonical basis

Let
\left(\mathscr{A}_{|Q|},\mathscr{X}_{|Q|}\right)
be the cluster ensemble associated to a quiver
Q
.
Recall the algebras
{\bf up}\left(\mathscr{A}_{|Q|}\right)
and
{\bf up}\left(\mathscr{X}_{|Q|}\right)
and the cluster modular group
\mathcal{G}_{|Q|}
. The Fock–Goncharov cluster duality conjecture asserts that

Conjecture 4.1

(
[
5
, Conjecture 4.1]
)
.

The algebra
{\bf up}(\mathscr{A}_{|Q|})
admits a canonical basis
\mathcal{G}_{|Q|}
-equivariantly parametrized by
\mathscr{X}_{|Q|}\left(\mathbb{Z}^{t}\right)
. The algebra
\mathbf{up}\left(\mathscr{X}_{|Q|}\right)
admits a canonical basis
\mathcal{G}_{|Q|}
-equivariantly parametrized by
\mathscr{A}_{|Q|}\left(\mathbb{Z}^{t}\right)
.

Conjecture
4.1
has been proved in
[
17
]
under two combinatorial assumptions, which can be summarized as follows.

Theorem 4.2

(
[
17
, Proposition 0.14]
)
.

Conjecture
4.1
holds if the following conditions are satisfied.

•

The non-frozen part of the quiver
Q
possesses a maximal green sequence or a reddening sequence.
8
8

8

Maximal green sequences and reddening sequences are special sequences of quiver mutations introduced by Keller
[
20
]
in order to study Donaldson–Thomas transformations. In the original statement of Gross–Hacking–Keel–Kontsevich, the theorem was stated with maximal green sequence; but they only need the existence of a maximal green sequence to ensure that the cluster complex does not lie within a half space, which is also a consequence of the existence of a reddening sequence.

•

The exchange matrix
\varepsilon=(\varepsilon_{ij})
of
Q
, with
i
running through the non-frozen vertices and
j
running through all the vertices, is of full rank.

Remark 4.3
.

For any quiver
Q
that satisfies the above combinatorial conditions, cluster duality gives rise to canonical bases of
{\bf up}\big(\mathscr{A}_{|Q|}\big)
and
{\bf up}\big(\mathscr{X}_{|Q|}\big)
, which we denote as follows:

\displaystyle\Theta_{\mathscr{A}}:=\big\{\theta_{q}\,\big|\,q\in\mathscr{X}_{|Q|}\big(\mathbb{Z}^{t}\big)\big\}\subset\mathbf{up}\big(\mathscr{A}_{|Q|}\big),

\displaystyle\Theta_{\mathscr{X}}:=\big\{\vartheta_{p}\,\big|\,p\in\mathscr{A}_{|Q|}\big(\mathbb{Z}^{t}\big)\big\}\subset\mathbf{up}\big(\mathscr{X}_{|Q|}\big).

The basis elements
\theta_{q}
and
\vartheta_{p}
satisfy many remarkable properties (see
[
17
]
for the comprehensive list).
One of them is that every
\theta_{q}\in\mathbf{up}\big(\mathscr{A}_{|Q|}\big)
in terms of the
K_{2}
cluster
\{A_{j}\}
associated to
Q
is expressed as

\theta_{q}=\prod_{j}A_{j}^{x_{j}}F\left(\left(\prod_{j}A_{j}^{\varepsilon_{kj}}\right)_{k\in I_{\operatorname{uf}}}\right),

(4.1)

where
(x_{j})
is the tropical coordinates of
q\in\mathscr{X}\big(\mathbb{Z}^{t}\big)
in terms of the Poisson cluster associated to the same quiver
Q
, and
F
is a polynomial with constant term 1 and variables of the form
\prod_{j}A_{j}^{\varepsilon_{kj}}
as
k
ranges through all unfrozen vertices. The elements
\vartheta_{p}
admit similar formulas.

One may notice that, on the one hand, we define
p^{*}(X_{i})=\prod_{j}A_{j}^{\varepsilon_{ji}}
in the definition of the
p
map, and on the other hand, the above polynomial
F
depends on
\prod_{j}A_{j}^{\varepsilon_{ij}}=p^{*}\big(X_{i}^{-1}\big)
. The reason this happens is that the cluster Poisson variables used in this paper are inverses of those used by Gross, Hacking, Keel, and Kontsevich in
[
17
]
. Such a switch frees us from considering tropicalization in which the tropical addition is maximum rather than minimum.

Proof of Theorem
1.2
.

For the quiver
Q_{a,n}
, the existence of a maximal green sequence was proved by Marsh and Scott in
[
22
]
and the existence of a cluster Donaldson–Thomas transformation (which is equivalent to a reddening sequence) was proved by Weng in
[
35
]
. By Lemma
2.12
, the second combinatorial condition holds. Hence Conjecture
4.1
holds for the cluster ensemble
(\mathscr{A}_{a,n},\mathscr{X}_{a,n})
. By Theorems
2.11
and
2.15
we know that
\mathbf{up}(\mathscr{A}_{a,n})\cong\mathcal{O}\big(\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}^{\times}(n)\big)
and
\mathbf{up}(\mathscr{X}_{a,n})\cong\mathcal{O}\big(\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)\big)
. This concludes the proof of Theorem
1.2
.
∎

Remark 4.4
.

This
\Theta
-basis was constructed by Gross, Hacking, Keel, and Kontsevich in
[
17
]
as formal power series by counting broken lines in scattering diagrams. To find an explicit procedure to determine all basis elements as regular functions and to compare them with other notable bases of representations (e.g., Lusztig’s canonical basis, Mirkovic–Vilonen basis, etc.) are interesting directions for future research.

4.2
Partial compactification, optimized quiver, and potential function

Let
Q=\big(I^{\operatorname{uf}}\subset I,\varepsilon\big)
be a quiver satifying the combinatorial conditions in Theorem
4.2
. Let
I^{0}:=I-I^{\operatorname{uf}}
be the set of frozen vertices.
For
i\in I^{0}
, let
D_{i}
denote the (irreducible) boundary divisor of
\mathscr{A}_{|Q|}
defined by setting
A_{i}=0
.
Let us glue
\mathscr{A}_{|Q|}
with these boundary divisors, obtaining the partially compactified space

\overline{\mathscr{A}}_{|Q|}:=\mathscr{A}_{|Q|}\cup\left(\bigcup_{i\in I^{0}}D_{i}\right).

This section is devoted to studying the ring
\mathcal{O}\big(\overline{\mathscr{A}}_{|Q|}\big)
of regular functions on
\overline{\mathscr{A}}_{|Q|}
.

Let
f\in\mathbf{up}\big(\mathscr{A}_{|Q|}\big)
. Denote by
{\rm ord}_{D_{i}}(f)
the order of
f
along the boundary divisor
D_{i}
. Note that
f
can be extended to a regular function on
D_{i}
if and only if
{\rm ord}_{D_{i}}(f)\geq 0
. Therefore

\mathcal{O}\big(\overline{\mathscr{A}}_{|Q|}\big)=\big\{f\in\mathbf{up}(\mathscr{A}_{|Q|})\,\big|\,{\rm ord}_{D_{i}}(f)\geq 0,\,\forall\,i\in I^{0}\big\}.

Recall the canonical basis
\Theta_{\mathscr{A}}
of
\mathcal{O}(\mathscr{A}_{|Q|})
. Consider the intersection

\Theta_{\overline{\mathscr{A}}}:=\Theta_{\mathscr{A}}\cap\mathcal{O}\left(\overline{\mathscr{A}}_{|Q|}\right)=\big\{\theta_{q}\in\Theta_{\mathscr{A}}\,\big|\,{\rm ord}_{D_{i}}(\theta_{q})\geq 0,\,\forall i\in I^{0}\big\}.

Conjecture 9.8 of
[
17
]
implies that the intersection
\Theta_{\overline{\mathscr{A}}}
descends to a linear basis of
\mathcal{O}\big(\overline{\mathscr{A}}_{|Q|}\big)
.
The paper
loc. cit.
provides a sufficient condition under which the aforementioned conjecture holds.

Definition 4.5
.

Let
i\in I^{0}
. If
\varepsilon_{ki}\geq 0
for all unfrozen vertices
k
, then we say the quiver
Q
is
optimized
for
i
. If there exists a mutation sequence
\tau
such that the mutated quiver
\tau Q
is optimized for
i
, then we say that
i
admits an optimized quiver in the equivalence class
|Q|
.

Remark 4.6
.

Because of the different conventions used in this paper vs. those in
[
17
]
, we define
Q
to be optimized for
i
if all arrows between
i
and unfrozen vertices point towards the unfrozen ones, as opposed to the other direction stated in
[
17
, Definition 9.1]
.

Proposition 4.7
.

If every frozen vertex
i
of
Q
admits an optimized quiver in
|Q|
, then the set
\Theta_{\overline{\mathscr{A}}}
forms a linear basis of
\mathcal{O}\big(\overline{\mathscr{A}}_{|Q|}\big)
.

Proof.

The linear independence of
\Theta_{\overline{\mathscr{A}}}
is clear.
Suppose that

f=\sum_{q\in\mathscr{X}(\mathbb{Z}^{t})}\alpha_{q}\theta_{q}\in\mathcal{O}\big(\overline{\mathscr{A}}_{|Q|}\big).

By definition,
{\rm ord}_{D_{i}}(f)\geq 0
for every frozen
i
. By
[
17
, Proposition 9.7]
, if
i
admits an optimized quiver in
|Q|
, then
{\rm ord}_{D_{i}}(\theta_{q})\geq 0
for all
q
with
\alpha_{q}\neq 0
. Therefore whenever the coefficient
\alpha_{q}
is nonzero, the function
\theta_{q}\in\Theta_{\overline{\mathscr{A}}}
. In other words, the set
\Theta_{\overline{\mathscr{A}}}
spans
\mathcal{O}\big(\overline{\mathscr{A}}_{|Q|}\big)
.
∎

Now it is natural to address the following question: for which
q\in\mathscr{X}_{|Q|}\big(\mathbb{Z}^{t}\big)
do we have
\theta_{q}\in\Theta_{\overline{\mathscr{A}}}
? A criterion for recognizing such
q
’s was suggested in
[
13
, Definition 12.7]
, and was proved in
[
17
]
.

Let
i\in I^{0}
. Let
p_{i}\in\mathscr{A}_{|Q|}\big(\mathbb{Z}^{t}\big)
be the tropical point such that its tropical coordinates
A_{j}^{t}(p_{i})=\delta_{ij}
, where
\delta_{ij}
is the Kronecker delta symbol. The existence and uniqueness of
p_{i}
is a direct consequence of the definition of cluster
K_{2}
mutations. Let
\vartheta_{p_{i}}\in\Theta_{\mathscr{X}}
be the theta function parametrized by
p_{i}
. Define the
GHKK potential

W:=\sum_{i\in I^{0}}\vartheta_{p_{i}}.

(4.2)

The following Proposition is a paraphrase of
[
17
, Lemma 9.3]
; we include it below for the sake of completeness.

Proposition 4.8
.

Assume that every frozen vertex of
Q
admits an optimized quiver in
|Q|
. Let
q\in\mathscr{X}_{|Q|}\big(\mathbb{Z}^{t}\big)
. Then
\theta_{q}\in\Theta_{\overline{\mathscr{A}}}
if and only if
W^{t}(q)\geq 0
.

Proof.

By the definition of tropicalization, we have

W^{t}(q)=\min_{i\in I^{0}}\big\{\vartheta_{p_{i}}^{t}(q)\big\}.

Therefore
W^{t}(q)\geq 0
if and only if every
\vartheta_{p_{i}}^{t}(q)\geq 0
. It suffices to show that if
i\in I^{0}
admits an optimized quiver, then

{\rm ord}_{D_{i}}(\theta_{q})=\vartheta_{p_{i}}^{t}(q).

(4.3)

Without loss of generality, let us assume that
Q
is optimized for
i
, i.e.,
\varepsilon_{ki}\geq 0
for all unfrozen vertices
k
.
Let
\{A_{j}\}
be the cluster of
\mathscr{A}_{|Q|}
associated to
Q
. Let
X_{i}
be the cluster variable of
\mathscr{X}_{|Q|}
associated to the vertex
i
in
Q
and let
x_{i}:=X_{i}^{t}
be its tropicalization. Since
\theta_{q}
is of the form (
4.1
) and
\epsilon_{ki}\geq 0
for all unfrozen vertices
k
, it follows that

{\rm ord}_{D_{i}}(\theta_{q})=x_{i}(q).

On the other hand, by
[
17
, Lemma 9.3]
, if
Q
is optimized for
i
, then
\vartheta_{p_{i}}=X_{i}
. Therefore
x_{i}(q)=X_{i}^{t}(q)=\vartheta_{p_{i}}^{t}(q)
, which concludes the proof of (
4.3
).
∎

Let us apply the above results to the cases of Grassmannians. It boils down to finding optimized quivers for frozen vertices in the quiver
Q_{a,n}
. As observed by L. Williams and stated in
[
17
, Proposition 9.4]
, the quiver
Q_{a,n}
is optimized for the vertices
(0,0)
and
(a,b)
; since the mutation sequence
\rho
in (
3.3
) rotates the frozen vertices of
Q_{a,n}
clockwise to their neighbors, by applying
\rho
repeatedly, each frozen vertex
i
has a chance to be at the position of
(0,0)
and therefore admits an optimized quiver. Indeed, the quiver
\rho^{n-i}Q_{a,n}
is optimized for the frozen vertex
i
.

The next Proposition shows that in the Grassmannian case, the potential function
\mathcal{W}=\sum_{i}\vartheta_{i}
in (
2.9
) coincides with the function
W
in (
4.2
).

Proposition 4.9
.

Under the algebra isomorphism
\mathbf{up}(\mathscr{X}_{a,n})\cong\mathcal{O}\big(\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)\big)
, the theta function
\vartheta_{p_{i}}
is identified with the function
\vartheta_{i}
defined in (
2.8
).

Proof.

Note that
Q_{a,n}
is optimized for
(0,0)
. By (
3.5
) and
[
17
, Lemma 9.3]
, we have

\vartheta_{n}=X_{0,0}=\vartheta_{p_{n}}.

For the other frozen vertices, let us apply the rotation mutation sequence
\rho
. Then

\vartheta_{p_{i}}=X_{n}^{\rho^{n-i}}=\big(R^{n-i}\big)^{*}X_{n}=\big(R^{n-i}\big)^{*}\vartheta_{n}=\vartheta_{i}.

∎

By Theorem
2.11
, or more precisely by the original version
[
32
, Theorem 3]
, the coordinate ring
\mathcal{O}(\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}(n))
is isomorphic to
\mathcal{O}\big(\overline{\mathscr{A}}_{a,n}\big)
. Combining Propositions
4.7
,
4.8
, and
4.9
, we get the following theorem.

Theorem 4.10
.

Under the isomorphisms
\mathcal{O}(\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}(n))\!\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{=}}\!\mathcal{O}\big(\overline{\mathscr{A}}_{a,n}\big)
and
\mathcal{O}\big(\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)\big)\!\cong\!\mathbf{up}(\mathscr{X}_{a,n})
,
the coordinate ring
\mathcal{O}(\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}(n))
admits a natural basis

\big\{\theta_{q}\,\big|\,q\in\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)\big(\mathbb{Z}^{t}\big),\,\mathcal{W}^{t}(q)\geq 0\big\}.

4.3
\boldsymbol{\mathbb{G}_{m}}
-action

Recall the
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-action on
\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}(n)
in (
2.1
). Let us restrict the
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-action to the open subset
\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}^{\times}(n)
. It induces a
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-action on
\mathcal{O}\big(\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}^{\times}(n)\big)
extending the one on
\mathcal{O}(\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}(n))
.

Recall the twisted monodromy function
P
on
\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)
.

Proposition 4.11
.

Let
q\in\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)\big(\mathbb{Z}^{t}\big)
. Its corresponding theta function
\theta_{q}\in\mathcal{O}\big(\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}^{\times}(n)\big)
is an eigenvector of the
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-action with weight
P^{t}(q)
:

t.\theta_{q}=t^{P^{t}(q)}\theta_{q}

Proof.

Let
\{A_{i,j}\}
be the
K_{2}
cluster of
\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}^{\times}(n)
associated to
Q_{a,n}
. By (
4.1
), the function
\theta_{q}
can be expressed as a Laurent polynomial

\theta_{q}=\prod_{(i,j)}A_{i,j}^{x_{i,j}}F\left(\left(\prod_{g}A_{g}^{\varepsilon_{fg}}\right)_{f\in I_{\operatorname{uf}}}\right),

(4.4)

where
(x_{i,j})
is the tropical coordinates of
q
with respect to the quiver
Q_{a,n}
, and
F
is a polynomial with constant term 1 and variables of the form
\prod_{g}A_{g}^{\varepsilon_{fg}}
for
f\in I_{\operatorname{uf}}
.

By definition, every
A_{f}
is a Plücker coordinate and therefore is of weight 1 with respect to the
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-action. Since
\sum_{f}\varepsilon_{fg}=0
for all
g\in I_{\operatorname{uf}}
by construction, the whole factor
F
is invariant under the
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-action. It implies that
\theta_{q}
is an eigenvector of weight
\sum_{(i,j)}x_{i,j}
. By tropicalizing
P=\prod_{f\in I}X_{f}
(Proposition
3.1
), we have

P^{t}(q)=\sum_{(i,j)}x_{i,j},

which concludes the proof.
∎

As a direct consequence, we get the following corollary.

Corollary 4.12
.

The representation
\mathcal{O}(\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}(n))_{c}=V_{c\omega_{a}}
has a canonical basis

\Theta(a,b,c):=\{\theta_{q}\,|\,q\in Q(a,b,c)\},

(4.5)

where
Q(a,b,c)
is defined in (
3.11
). By combining this result with Proposition
3.8
we deduce that there is a natural bijection between
\Theta(a,b,c)
and the set of plane partitions
P(a,b,c)
.

Remark 4.13
.

Rietsch and Williams proved a similar result on parametrization of a basis of
V_{c\omega_{a}}
by plane partitions
[
29
, Lemma 16.16]
, which also relies on cluster duality. However, they approached the problem from the other direction by realizing the open positroid variety
\operatorname{Gr}_{a}^{\times}(n)
as a cluster Poisson variety and choosing basis elements from the canonical basis of
\mathbf{up}(\mathscr{X})
, which is parametrized by
\mathscr{A}\big(\mathbb{Z}^{t}\big)
. In order to do this, they break the cyclic symmetry of Grassmannian by fixing a particular Plücker coordinate, denoted by
P_{\max}
in
[
29
, Section 1.6]
. As a consequence, the basis in
[
29
, Lemma 16.16]
does not respect the twisted cyclic rotation on Grassmannian. In contrast, the basis
\Theta(a,b,c)
in Corollary
4.12
is invariant under twisted cyclic rotation, which is crucial to our proof of a cyclic sieving phenomenon of plane partitions (Theorem
1.6
).

In this paper, the basis elements in (
4.5
) are realized as functions on the decorated Grassmannian
\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}(n)
, as opposed to the ordinary Grassmannian in
loc. cit
. And the space
\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}(n)
is realized as a cluster
K_{2}
variety, as opposed to the space
\operatorname{Gr}_{a}^{\times}(n)
as a cluster Poisson variety in
loc. cit
. Our basis
\Theta(a,b,c)
has an invariance property with respect to the twisted cyclic rotation
C_{a}
, which plays a crucial role in our proof of cyclic sieving phenomenon.
Moreover, we will prove in the next section that our basis also fits into the weight space decomposition of
V_{c\omega_{a}}
, which is a stronger result.

4.4
Torus action and weight space decomposition

By (
2.3
),
\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}^{\times}(n)\cong\operatorname{SL}_{a}\backslash\operatorname{Mat}_{a,n}^{\times}
.
The group
{\rm GL}_{n}
acts on the right of
\operatorname{Mat}_{a,n}^{\times}
by matrix multiplication. The maximal torus

T=(\mathbb{G}_{m})^{n}\subset{\rm GL}_{n}
of diagonal matrices acts by rescaling the column vectors
v_{i}
of the matrices in
\operatorname{Mat}_{a,n}^{\times}

(v_{1},\dots,v_{n}).(t_{1},\dots,t_{n}):=(t_{1}v_{1},t_{2}v_{2},\dots,t_{n}v_{n}).

Its induced left
\left(\mathbb{G}_{m}\right)^{n}
-action on
\mathcal{O}\big(\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}^{\times}(n)\big)
gives rise to a weight space decomposition

\mathcal{O}\big(\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}^{\times}(n)\big)=\bigoplus_{\mu}\mathcal{O}(\mu),

where
\mu=(\mu_{1},\dots,\mu_{n})\in\mathbb{Z}^{n}
and
\mathcal{O}(\mu)
consists of the functions
F
such that

(t_{1},\dots,t_{n}).F=t_{1}^{\mu_{1}}\cdots t_{n}^{\mu_{n}}F.

In particular, if we restrict to the representation
V_{c\omega_{a}}=\mathcal{O}(\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}(n))_{c}
, then we get the weight space decomposition

V_{c\omega_{a}}=\bigoplus_{\mu}V_{c\omega_{a}}(\mu),\qquad\mbox{where}\quad V_{c\omega_{a}}(\mu):=V_{c\omega_{a}}\cap\mathcal{O}(\mu).

In this section, we show that the theta basis
\Theta(a,b,c)
is compatible with the weight space decomposition of the representation
V_{c\omega_{a}}
.

Recall the following dual torus projection defined in (
2.11
)

M=(M_{1},\dots,M_{n})\colon\ {\rm Conf}_{n}^{\times}(a)\longrightarrow T^{\vee}.

Let us tropicalize the map
M
, obtaining

M^{t}=\big(M_{1}^{t},\dots,M_{n}^{t}\big)\colon\ \mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)\big(\mathbb{Z}^{t}\big)\longrightarrow T^{\vee}\big(\mathbb{Z}^{t}\big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\sim}}{{=}}\mathbb{Z}^{n}.

Proposition 4.14
.

Let
q\in\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)
. The theta function
\theta_{q}
is an eigenvector of the
T
-action on
\mathcal{O}\big(\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}^{\times}(n)\big)
with weight
M^{t}(q)
, i.e.,

(t_{1},\dots,t_{n}).\theta_{q}=t_{1}^{M_{1}^{t}(q)}\cdots t_{n}^{M_{n}^{t}(q)}\theta_{q}.

The proof of Proposition
4.14
will require a little preparation.
Recall the
a
-element set
I(i,j)
assigned to each vertex
(i,j)
of the quiver
Q_{a,n}
(
2.20
):

I(i,j)=\{\underbrace{b-j+1,\dots,b-j+i}_{\text{$i$ indices}},\underbrace{b+i+1,\dots,n}_{\text{$a-i$ indices}}\}.

For
k\in\{1,\dots,n\}
, let
F_{k}
denote the collection of vertices
(i,j)
in
Q_{a,n}
such that
k\in I(i,j)
.
By the definition of
I(i,j)
, it is easy to check that the sets
F_{k}
are of two patterns.
When
1\leq k\leq b
, the vertices in
F_{k}
are enclosed in a stair-shape diagram, such that the difference between the consecutive steps is 1, until its height becomes
0
or until it touches the rightmost column. When
b<k\leq n
, we get a smaller stair with another part that consists of the
(0,0)
vertex and possibly a rectangle:

\ddots
F_{k}
(1,b-k+1)
(2,b-k+2)
1\leq k\leq b

\ddots
F_{k}
F_{k}
(k-b,1)
(0,0)
(1,b)
b\leq k\leq n

.

(a,n)=(2,5)

F_{k}

Lemma 4.16
.

Recall the clusters
\{X_{i,j}\}
of
\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)
associated to
Q_{a,n}
in (
2.29
). The function

M_{k}=\prod_{(i,j)\in F_{k}}X_{i,j}.

Proof.

We will prove the lemma under the assumption that
a\leq b
(the cases with
a\geq b
are completely analogous). When
1\leq k\leq a
, we have

\prod_{(i,j)\in F_{k}}X_{i,j}=\frac{L_{1,b-k+1}}{L_{1,b-k+2}}\frac{L_{2,b-k+2}}{L_{2,b-k+3}}\cdots\frac{L_{k-1,b-1}}{L_{k-1,b}}L_{k,b}=\frac{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{k}L_{i,b-k+i}}{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{k-1}L_{i,b-k+i+1}}

Then by Lemma
3.10
,

p^{*}\left(\frac{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{k}L_{i,b-k+i}}{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{k-1}L_{i,b-k+i+1}}\right)=\frac{\Delta_{\{k-a,\dots,k-1\}}}{\Delta_{\{k-a+1,\dots,k\}}}\prod_{i=k-a}^{k-1}\lambda_{i}=p^{*}(M_{k}),

where the last equality follows from the definition of
M_{k}
in (
2.10
) (please keep in mind that all indices of
\lambda
and
\Delta
are taken modulo
n
). When
a<k\leq b
, we get

\prod_{(i,j)\in F_{k}}X_{i,j}=\frac{L_{1,b-k+1}}{L_{1,b-k+2}}\frac{L_{2,b-k+2}}{L_{2,b-k+3}}\cdots\frac{L_{a,n-k}}{L_{a,n-k+1}}=\frac{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{a}L_{i,b-k+i}}{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{a}L_{i,b-k+i+1}}.

Again by Lemma
3.10
,

p^{*}\left(\frac{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{a}L_{i,b-k+i}}{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{a}L_{i,b-k+i+1}}\right)=\frac{\Delta_{\{k-a,\dots,k-1\}}}{\Delta_{\{k-a+1,\dots,k\}}}\prod_{i=k-a}^{k-1}\lambda_{i}=p^{*}(M_{k}).

Lastly, when
b<k\leq n
, we have

p^{*}\left(\prod_{(i,j)\in F_{k}}X_{i,j}\right)=p^{*}\left(L_{0,0}\frac{\prod\limits_{i=1}^{n-k}L_{k-b+i,i}}{\prod\limits_{i=0}^{n-k}L_{k-b+i,i+1}}\right)=\frac{\Delta_{\{k-a,\dots,k-1\}}}{\Delta_{\{k-a+1,\dots,k\}}}\prod_{i=k-a}^{k-1}\lambda_{i}=p^{*}\left(M_{k}\right).

Proof of Proposition
4.14
.

The proof makes use of the expression (
4.4
) of
\theta_{q}
again. For a nonfrozen vertex
f
, the product
\prod_{g}A_{g}^{\varepsilon_{fg}}=p^{*}\big(X_{f}^{-1}\big)
is independent of the rescaling of the column vectors
v_{i}
due to the well-defined-ness of the unfrozen variable
X_{f}
. Therefore the polynomial
F
in (
4.4
) is invariant under the rescaling
T
-action. For the Plücker coordinates
A_{i,j}
, note that it is affected by the
t_{k}
component of
T
if and only if
(i,j)\in F_{k}
. Therefore

(t_{1},\dots,t_{n}).\theta_{q}=t_{1}^{\mu_{1}}t_{2}^{\mu_{2}}\cdots t_{n}^{\mu_{n}}\theta_{q},\qquad\mbox{where}\quad\mu_{k}=\sum_{(i,j)\in F_{k}}x_{i,j}.

By Lemma
4.16
, we get
\sum\limits_{(i,j)\in F_{k}}x_{i,j}=M_{k}^{t}(q)
.
∎

Combining Corollary
4.12
with Proposition
4.14
, we get the following result.

Corollary 4.17
.

The weight space
V_{c\omega_{a}}(\mu)
has a canonical basis

\Theta(a,b,c)(\mu):=\big\{\theta_{q}\in\Theta(a,b,c)\,\big|\,M^{t}(q)=\mu\big\}.

Recall that
\Theta(a,b,c)
is parametrized by the set
P(a,b,c)
of plane partitions.
In the rest of this section, we present a concrete decomposition of
P(a,b,c)
compatible with the above decomposition of
\Theta(a,b,c)
.

Recall that the Gelfand–Zetlin patterns
[
11
]
for
{\rm GL}_{n}
are triangular arrays of integers with non-increasing rows and columns as follows

\Lambda=\left(\begin{matrix}\lambda_{1,1}&\lambda_{1,2}&\lambda_{1,3}&\cdots&\lambda_{1,n}\\
&\lambda_{2,2}&\lambda_{2,3}&\cdots&\lambda_{2,n}\\
&&\lambda_{3,3}&\cdots&\lambda_{3,n}\\
&&&\ddots&\vdots\\
&&&&\lambda_{n,n}\end{matrix}\right).

Let
\delta_{i}:=\sum\limits_{k=1}^{i}\lambda_{k,n-i+k}
be the sums of entries along diagonals.
Define

\operatorname{wt}(\Lambda):=(\delta_{1},\delta_{2}-\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{n}-\delta_{n-1}).

(4.6)

Now let
\pi=(\pi_{ij})\in P(a,b,c)
. Note that
0\leq\pi_{i,j}\leq c
. It uniquely determines a Gelfand–Zetlin pattern as follows

\Lambda_{\pi}:=\left(\begin{matrix}c&c&\cdots&c&\pi_{1,1}&\pi_{1,2}&\cdots&\pi_{1,b}\\
&c&\cdots&c&\pi_{2,1}&\pi_{2,2}&\cdots&\pi_{2,b}\\
&&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
&&&c&\pi_{a,1}&\pi_{a,2}&\cdots&\pi_{a,b}\\
&&&&0&0&\cdots&0\\
&&&&&0&\cdots&0\\
&&&&&&\ddots&\vdots\\
&&&&&&&0\end{matrix}\right).

Consider the decomposition

P(a,b,c)=\bigsqcup_{\mu}P(a,b,c)(\mu),\qquad\mbox{where}\quad P(a,b,c)(\mu):=\{\pi\in P(a,b,c)\,|\,\operatorname{wt}(\Lambda_{\pi})=\mu\}.

Proposition 4.18
.

The basis
\Theta(a,b,c)(\mu)
is in natural bijection with
P(a,b,c)(\mu)
.

Proof.

Recall the tropical Gelfand–Zetlin coordinates
\{l_{i,j}\}
of
\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)\big(\mathbb{Z}^{t}\big)
. Let us arrange them in the following triangular pattern

\begin{matrix}l_{0,0}&l_{0,0}&\cdots&l_{0,0}&l_{1,1}&l_{1,2}&\cdots&l_{1,b}\\
&l_{0,0}&\cdots&l_{0,0}&l_{2,1}&l_{2,2}&\cdots&l_{2,b}\\
&&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
&&&l_{0,0}&l_{a,1}&l_{a,2}&\cdots&l_{a,b}\\
&&&&0&0&\cdots&0\\
&&&&&0&\cdots&0\\
&&&&&&\ddots&\vdots\\
&&&&&&&0\end{matrix}

Consider the sums of
l_{i,j}
along each diagonal:

\delta_{1}=l_{1,b},\quad\delta_{2}=l_{1,b-1}+l_{2,b},\quad\dots,\quad\delta_{n}=al_{0,0}.

Following the same argument as in the proof of Lemma
4.16
, we get

M^{t}=\big(M_{1}^{t},M_{2}^{t},\dots,M_{n}^{t}\big)=(\delta_{1},\delta_{2}-\delta_{1},\dots,\delta_{n}-\delta_{n-1}).

(4.7)

The proposition then follows from a comparison between (
4.7
) and (
4.6
).
∎

5
Cyclic sieving phenomenon of plane partitions

As an application of cluster duality for Grassmannians, we use the basis for
V_{c\omega_{a}}
obtained in Corollary
4.12
to prove a cyclic sieving phenomenon of plane partitions under the sequence of toggles
\eta
defined in Section
1.2
.

First we recall that the parametrization of basis we obtained from cluster duality is equivariant with respect to the cluster modular group action (Theorem
1.2
). Since both the twisted cyclic rotation
C_{a}
on
\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}^{\times}(n)
and the rotation
R
on
\mathscr{C}\!{\rm onf}_{n}^{\times}(a)
come from the same cluster modular group element (Section
3.2
), it follows that

C_{a}^{*}\theta_{q}=\theta_{R^{t}(q)}.

On the other hand, we discovered a natural bijection between the parametrization set
Q(a,b,c)\!
of the basis
\{\theta_{q}\,|\,q\in Q(a,b,c)\}
of
V_{c\omega_{a}}
and the set of plane partitions
P(a,b,c)
using tropical Gelfand–Zetlin coordinates (Proposition
3.8
). We claimed in Theorem
1.7
that the action of
C_{a}
on the basis element
\theta_{q}
is equivalent to the sequence of toggles
\eta
on plane partitions, so let us first use Gelfand–Zetlin coordinates once more to prove Theorem
1.7
.

We begin by setting
L_{i,0}=L_{0,j}=P
and
L_{a+1,j}=L_{i,n-a+1}=1
.
Let

L_{i,j}^{\prime}:=R^{*}L_{i,j},

where
R^{*}
denotes the pull-back via the biregular morphism
R
. Note that
R^{*}P=P
. Therefore
L_{i,0}^{\prime}=L_{0,j}^{\prime}=P
and
L_{a+1,j}^{\prime}=L_{i,n-a+1}^{\prime}=1
.

Lemma 5.1
.

We have

L^{\prime}_{i,j}=\frac{\big(L^{\prime}_{i,j-1}+L_{i-1,j}\big)L^{\prime}_{i+1,j}L_{i,j+1}}{L_{i,j}\big(L^{\prime}_{i+1,j}+L_{i,j+1}\big)},\qquad\forall\,1\leq i\leq a,\quad\forall\,1\leq j\leq b.

Proof.

Throughout the proof we will adopt the convention of using a prime superscript to denote the coordinates after the cyclic rotation action. Let

A^{\prime}_{i,j}:=C_{a}^{*}A_{i,j}=\Delta_{I^{\prime}(i,j)},
with
I^{\prime}(i,j)
defined as (
3.4
):

I^{\prime}(i,j)=\{\underbrace{b-j,\dots,b-j+i-1}_{\text{consecutive $i$ indices}},\underbrace{b+i,\dots,n-1}_{\text{consecutive $a-i$ indices}}\}

By Lemma
3.10
we get

p^{*}\left(L^{\prime}_{i,j}\right)=\frac{A^{\prime}_{i-1,j-1}}{A^{\prime}_{i,j}}\prod_{k=i-a}^{b-j}\lambda^{\prime}_{k}=\frac{A^{\prime}_{i-1,j-1}}{A^{\prime}_{i,j}}\prod_{k=i-a-1}^{b-j-1}\lambda_{k}.

The last equality is obtained from the fact that
\lambda^{\prime}_{k}=\lambda_{k-1}
for all
k
, which is due to the cyclic rotation. Using
I(i,j)
(
2.20
) and
I^{\prime}(i,j)
(
3.4
) one can deduce the following identity from the Plücker relations (
2.3
):

\displaystyle A^{\prime}_{i-1,j-2}A_{i-1,j}+A_{i-2,j-1}A^{\prime}_{i,j-1}=A^{\prime}_{i-1,j-1}A_{i-1,j-1},

\displaystyle A^{\prime}_{i,j-1}A_{i,j+1}+A_{i-1,j}A^{\prime}_{i+1,j}=A^{\prime}_{i,j}A_{i,j}.

Let us take the ratio of them and multiply by
\prod\limits_{k=i-a}^{b-j}\lambda_{k}\lambda_{k-1}
on both sides. The right hand side is

{\rm r.h.s.}=\left(\frac{A^{\prime}_{i-1,j-1}}{A^{\prime}_{i,j}}\prod_{k=i-a-1}^{b-j-1}\lambda_{k}\right)\left(\frac{A_{i-1,j-1}}{A_{i,j}}\prod_{k=i-a}^{b-j}\lambda_{k}\right)=p^{*}\big(L_{i,j}^{\prime}L_{i,j}\big).

The left hand side becomes

\displaystyle{\rm l.h.s.}

\displaystyle=\frac{\big(A^{\prime}_{i-1,j-2}A_{i-1,j}+A_{i-2,j-1}A^{\prime}_{i,j-1}\big)/\big(A_{i-1,j}A^{\prime}_{i,j-1}\big)}{\big(A^{\prime}_{i,j-1}A_{i,j+1}+A_{i-1,j}A^{\prime}_{i+1,j}\big)/\big(A_{i-1,j}A^{\prime}_{i,j-1}\big)}\cdot\prod_{k=i-a}^{b-j}\lambda_{k}\lambda_{k-1}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{A^{\prime}_{i-1,j-2}}{A^{\prime}_{i,j-1}}+\frac{A_{i-2,j-1}}{A_{i-1,j}}\right)\cdot\left(\frac{A_{i,j+1}}{A_{i-1,j}}+\frac{A^{\prime}_{i+1,j}}{A^{\prime}_{i,j-1}}\right)^{-1}\cdot\prod_{k=i-a}^{b-j}\lambda_{k}\lambda_{k-1}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{A^{\prime}_{i-1,j-2}}{A^{\prime}_{i,j-1}}\prod_{k=i-a-1}^{b-j}\lambda_{k}+\frac{A_{i-2,j-1}}{A_{i-1,j}}\prod_{k=i-a-1}^{b-j}\lambda_{k}\right)

\displaystyle\hphantom{=}{}\times\left(\frac{A_{i,j+1}}{A_{i-1,j}\prod\limits_{k={i-a}}^{b-j-1}\lambda_{k}}+\frac{A^{\prime}_{i+1,j}}{A^{\prime}_{i,j-1}\prod\limits_{k={i-a}}^{b-j-1}\lambda_{k}}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=p^{*}\left(\big(L^{\prime}_{i,j-1}+L_{i-1,j}\big)\cdot\big(L_{i,j+1}^{-1}+L_{i+1,j}^{\prime-1}\big)^{-1}\right)=p^{*}\left(\frac{\big(L^{\prime}_{i,j-1}+L_{i-1,j}\big)L^{\prime}_{i+1,j}L_{i,j+1}}{\big(L^{\prime}_{i+1,j}+L_{i,j+1}\big)}\right),

which is precisely the right hand side.
∎

Lemma
5.1
allows us to compute
L^{\prime}_{i,j}
recursively. Let
\Pi=(\Pi_{ij})
be a matrix such that its rows are numbered
0,\dots,a+1
and its columns are numbered
0,\dots,b+1
. For
1\leq i\leq a
and
1\leq j\leq b
, we define a
birational toggling
action
\tau_{i,j}
sending
\Pi
to the matrix
\tau_{i,j}\Pi
such that

(\tau_{i,j}\Pi)_{k,l}:=\begin{cases}\Pi_{k,l}&\text{if $(k,l)\neq(i,j)$},\\
\displaystyle\frac{(\Pi_{i,j-1}+\Pi_{i-1,j})\Pi_{i+1,j}\Pi_{i,j+1}}{\Pi_{i,j}(\Pi_{i+1,j}+\Pi_{i,j+1})}&\text{if $(k,l)=(i,j)$}.\end{cases}

Recall the toggling sequence
\eta
in (
1.1
).

Lemma 5.2
.

Let us apply the toggling sequence
\eta
to the initial
(a+2)\times(b+2)
matrix

\Pi=\begin{pmatrix}P&P&P&\cdots&P&1\\
P&L_{1,1}&L_{1,2}&\cdots&L_{1,b}&1\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
P&L_{a,1}&L_{a,2}&\cdots&L_{a,b}&1\\
1&1&1&\cdots&1&1\end{pmatrix}.

Then the matrix resulting from the application of
\eta
is

\eta\Pi=\begin{pmatrix}P&P&P&\cdots&P&1\\
P&L^{\prime}_{1,1}&L^{\prime}_{1,2}&\cdots&L^{\prime}_{1,n-a}&1\\
\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
P&L^{\prime}_{a,1}&L^{\prime}_{a,2}&\cdots&L^{\prime}_{a,n-a}&1\\
1&1&1&\cdots&1&1\end{pmatrix}.

In other words, the pull-back of the Gelfand–Zetlin coordinates via the rotation
R
is given by
\eta
.

Proof.

Note that the sequence
\eta
toggles at each (internal) entry exactly once. It suffices to prove that the step
\tau_{i,j}
within the sequence
\eta
changes
L_{i,j}
to
L^{\prime}_{i,j}
. This follows from the fact that the toggling sequence
\eta
goes from bottom to top within each column and from left to right through all columns. So when we toggle at the entry
(i,j)
, the matrix entry to the left and the matrix entry below have already been changed to
L^{\prime}_{i,j-1}
and
L^{\prime}_{i+1,j}
respectively. The rest of the proof is just a straightforward comparison between the toggling formula and Lemma
5.1
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
1.7
.

Set
l^{\prime}_{i,j}:=(L^{\prime}_{i,j})^{t}=\big(R^{t}\big)^{*}(l_{i,j})
. By Lemma
5.2
, with the convention that
l_{0,j}=l_{i,0}=l_{0,0}=c
and
l_{a+1,j}=l_{i,b+1}=0
, we see that
l^{\prime}_{i,j}
can be computed recursively as

l^{\prime}_{0,0}=l_{0,0}=c

and for
(i,j)\neq(0,0)
,

\displaystyle l^{\prime}_{i,j}

\displaystyle=\min\{l^{\prime}_{i,j-1},l_{i-1,j}\}+l^{\prime}_{i+1}+l_{i,j+1}-\min\{l^{\prime}_{i+1,j},l_{i,j+1}\}-l_{i,j}

\displaystyle=\min\{l^{\prime}_{i,j-1},l_{i-1,j}\}+\max\{l^{\prime}_{i+1,j},l_{i,j+1}\}-l_{i,j}.

(5.1)

In the process of computing
l^{\prime}_{i,j}
, the coordinates below and to the left of
l_{i,j}
has been toggled in the way the mutation sequence is constructed. Therefore the formula (
5.1
) recovers the toggling formula (
1.1
). It concludes the proof of Theorem
1.7
.
∎

Now let us deduce the cyclic sieving phenomenon of plane partitions claimed in Theorem
1.6
from Theorem
1.7
.

Proof of Theorem
1.6
.

Finite dimensional irreducible representations of
\operatorname{GL}_{n}
are classified by their highest weights, which are
n
-tuples of integers
\lambda=\left(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n}\right)
with
\lambda_{1}\geq\lambda_{2}\geq\dots\geq\lambda_{n}
. Denote by
V_{\lambda}
the finite dimension irreducible representation with highest weight
\lambda
(see, e.g.,
[
8
, Lecture 15]
). Let
D(p,q)
be the diagonal matrix
\operatorname{diag}\big(pq^{n-1},\dots,pq,p\big)
. We compute the trace of the action of
D(p,q)
on
V_{\lambda}
as

\operatorname{Tr}_{V_{\lambda}}D(p,q)=p^{\left\langle\omega_{n},\lambda\right\rangle}\sum_{\mu}\dim V_{\lambda}(\mu)q^{\left\langle\rho,\mu\right\rangle},

where
V_{\lambda}(\mu)
is the
\mu
-weight subspace of
V_{\lambda}
,
\rho
is the dominant weight
(n-1,\dots,2,1,0)
, and
\omega_{n}=(1,\dots,1)
.

Let
\zeta={\rm e}^{2\pi\sqrt{-1}/n}
. The characteristic polynomial of the matrix
C_{a}
in (
1.2
) is

\det\left(t\,\mathrm{Id}_{n}-C_{a}\right)=t^{n}-(-1)^{a-1}.

It has
n
-distinct roots
\zeta^{-\frac{a-1}{2}},\zeta^{-\frac{a-1}{2}}\zeta,\dots,\zeta^{-\frac{a-1}{2}}\zeta^{n-1}
over
\mathbb{C}
. Therefore
C_{a}
is conjugate to
D\big(\zeta^{-\frac{a-1}{2}},\zeta\big)
and
C_{a}^{k}
is conjugate to
D\big(\zeta^{-\frac{(a-1)k}{2}},\zeta^{k}\big)
. By Theorem
1.7
, the number of plane partitions fixed by
\eta^{k}
is equal to the number of basis vectors in
\{\theta_{\pi}\}_{\pi\in P(a,b,c)}
fixed by
C_{a}^{k}
. Therefore

\displaystyle\#\left\{\pi\,\Big|\,\begin{matrix}\pi\in P(a,b,c)\\
\eta^{k}(\pi)=\pi\end{matrix}\right\}

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Tr}_{V_{c\omega_{a}}}C_{a}^{k}=\operatorname{Tr}_{V_{c\omega_{a}}}D\big(\zeta^{-\frac{(a-1)k}{2}},\zeta^{k}\big)

\displaystyle=\big(\zeta^{k}\big)^{-\frac{(a-1)ac}{2}}\sum_{\mu}\dim V_{c\omega_{a}}(\mu)\big(\zeta^{k}\big)^{\left\langle\rho,\mu\right\rangle}.

By Proposition
4.18
, we have

{\dim}V_{c\omega_{a}}(\mu)=\#P(a,b,c)(\mu).

Let
\pi\in P(a,b,c)
. By using (
4.6
) and setting
\delta_{0}=0
we get

\left\langle\rho,\operatorname{wt}(\Lambda_{\pi})\right\rangle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}(n-k)(\delta_{k}-\delta_{k-1})=\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}\delta_{k}=\frac{(a-1)ac}{2}+\sum_{i,j}\pi_{i,j}=\frac{(a-1)ac}{2}+|\pi|.

Therefore

\displaystyle\big(\zeta^{k}\big)^{-\frac{(a-1)ac}{2}}\sum_{\mu}\dim V_{c\omega_{a}}(\mu)\big(\zeta^{k}\big)^{\left\langle\rho,\mu\right\rangle}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mu}\sum_{\pi\in P(a,b,c)(\mu)}\big(\zeta^{k}\big)^{|\pi|}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pi\in P(a,b,c)}\big(\zeta^{k}\big)^{|\pi|}=M_{a,b,c}\big(\zeta^{k}\big).

∎

Appendix A
Generalities on cluster ensembles

We briefly recall the definition of cluster ensembles following
[
5
]
.

Quiver mutations.

Let
Q=\big(I^{\operatorname{uf}}\subset I,\varepsilon\big)
be a quiver without loops or 2-cycles:
I
is the set of vertices,
I^{\operatorname{uf}}
is the set of unfrozen vertices, and
\varepsilon
is an
I\times I
skew-symmetric matrix called the
exchange matrix
encoding the data of number of arrows between vertices

\varepsilon_{ij}=\#\{j\rightarrow i\}-\#\{i\rightarrow j\}.

For the rest of this appendix we let
m=\#I
and
l=\#I^{\operatorname{uf}}
.

Given a quiver
Q
, the
quiver mutation

\mu_{k}
at a non-frozen vertex
k\in I^{\operatorname{uf}}
creates a new quiver
\mu_{k}(Q)
by the following procedure

1.

For each pair of arrows
i\longrightarrow k\longrightarrow j
, create a “composite” arrow
i\longrightarrow j
.

2.

Reverse all arrows incident to
k
.

3.

Remove any maximal disjoint collection of oriented 2-cycles.

This quiver mutation is involutive:
\mu_{k}^{2}(Q)=Q
. Repeating the process at every non-frozen vertex for each new quiver obtained via quiver mutations, we get an infinite
l
-valent tree

\mathbb{T}_{l}
such that every vertex
t
of
\mathbb{T}_{l}
is assigned a quiver
Q_{t}=\big(I^{\operatorname{uf}}\subset I,\varepsilon_{t}\big)
.

Cluster ensembles.

Now assign to each vertex
t
two coordinate charts: the
K_{2}
cluster
\alpha_{t}=\{A_{i,t}\,|\,i\in I\}
and the Poisson cluster
\chi_{t}=\{X_{i,t}\,|\,i\in I\}
. Geometrically, they correspond to a pair of algebraic tori:

\mathcal{T}_{t,\alpha}={\rm Spec}\big(\mathds{k}[A_{1,t}^{\pm},\dots,A_{m,t}^{\pm}]\big),\qquad\mathcal{T}_{t,\chi}={\rm Spec}\big(\mathds{k}[X_{1,t}^{\pm},\dots,X_{m,t}^{\pm}]\big).

(A.1)

There is a homomorphism
p
relating them:

p^{*}X_{i,t}=\prod_{j\in I}A_{j,t}^{\varepsilon_{ji,t}}.

(A.2)

The transition maps between the pairs of tori assigned to
Q_{t}
and
Q_{t^{\prime}}=\mu_{k}(Q_{t})
are as follows:

\displaystyle\mu_{k}^{*}A_{i,t^{\prime}}=\begin{cases}\displaystyle A_{k,t}^{-1}\left(\prod_{j\in I}A_{j,t}^{[\varepsilon_{jk,t}]_{+}}+\prod_{j\in I}A_{j,t}^{[-\varepsilon_{jk,t}]_{+}}\right)&\mbox{if $i=k$},\\
A_{i,t}&\mbox{if $i\neq k$},\\
\end{cases}

(A.3)

\displaystyle\mu_{k}^{*}X_{i,t^{\prime}}=\begin{cases}X_{k,t}^{-1}&\mbox{if $i=k$},\\
X_{i,t}\big(1+X_{k,t}^{{\rm sgn}(\varepsilon_{ik,t})}\big)^{\varepsilon_{ik,t}}&\mbox{if $i\neq k$}.\\
\end{cases}

(A.4)

Here
[\varepsilon]_{+}=\max\{\varepsilon,0\}
.

Let us glue all the algebraic tori via the transitions (
A.3
), (
A.4
), obtaining a pair of varieties called a cluster ensemble

\mathscr{A}_{|Q|}=\bigcup_{t}\mathcal{T}_{t,\alpha},\qquad\mathscr{X}_{|Q|}=\bigcup_{t}\mathcal{T}_{t,\chi}.

(A.5)

where
t
runs through all the vertices of the tree
\mathbb{T}_{l}
. The map
p
in (
A.2
) is compatible with the transition maps (
A.3
)(
A.4
). Therefore we get a natural map

p\colon\ \mathscr{A}_{|Q|}\longrightarrow\mathscr{X}_{|Q|}.

(A.6)

In general the map
p
is neither injective nor surjective.

The coordinate rings of these varieties are the algebras of universal Laurent polynomials

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(\mathscr{A}_{|Q|})={\bf up}(\mathscr{A}_{|Q|}):=\bigcap_{t}\mathds{k}\big[A_{1,t}^{\pm},\dots,A_{m,t}^{\pm}\big],

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}(\mathscr{X}_{|Q|})={\bf up}(\mathscr{X}_{|Q|}):=\bigcap_{t}\mathds{k}\big[X_{1,t}^{\pm},\dots,X_{m,t}^{\pm}\big].

Cluster modular group.
To each torus
\mathcal{T}_{t,\alpha}
in (
A.1
) is associated a differential form

\Omega_{t}:=\sum_{i,j}\varepsilon_{ij,t}\frac{{\rm d}A_{i,t}}{A_{i,t}}\wedge\frac{{\rm d}A_{j,t}}{A_{j,t}}.

This
\Omega_{t}
is compatible with the transition (
A.3
) and therefore can be lifted to a global differential form
\Omega
on
\mathscr{A}_{|Q|}
. A
cluster automorphism

\tau
of
\mathscr{A}_{|Q|}
is a biregular isomorphism of
\mathscr{A}_{|Q|}
such that

•

It preserves the differential form:
\tau^{*}\Omega=\Omega
.

•

For every
K_{2}
-cluster
\alpha_{t}=\{A_{i,t}\}
, the pullback
\tau^{*}(\alpha_{t}):=\{\tau^{*}A_{i,t}\}
remains a
K_{2}
-cluster, with its indices
i
possibly permuted.

Locally,
\tau
can be realized by a sequence of mutations that sends a quiver
Q
to itself up to permutations of vertices.
The
cluster modular group

\mathcal{G}_{\mathscr{A},|Q|}
consists of cluster automorphisms of
\mathscr{A}_{|Q|}
.

To each torus
\mathcal{T}_{t,\chi}
is associated a bi-vector

B_{t}:=\sum_{i,j}\varepsilon_{ij,t}X_{i,t}\frac{\partial}{\partial X_{i,t}}\wedge X_{j,t}\frac{\partial}{\partial X_{j,t}},

which can be lifted to a global bi-vector
B
on
\mathscr{X}_{|{Q}|}
. A cluster automorphism of
\mathscr{X}_{|Q|}
is a biregular isomorphism of
\mathscr{X}_{|Q|}
that preserves the bi-vector
B
and permutes its Poisson clusters. Denote by
\mathcal{G}_{\mathscr{X},|Q|}
the group of cluster automorphisms of
\mathscr{X}_{|Q|}
.

From the tropical cluster duality proved by Nakanishi and Zelevinsky
[
25
]
one can deduce that the cluster modular group
\mathcal{G}_{\mathscr{X},|Q|}=\mathcal{G}_{\mathscr{A},|Q|}
. Hence, we will drop the subscripts
\mathscr{A}
and
\mathscr{X}
in the notation and denote it by
\mathcal{G}_{|Q|}
.

Quiver extensions.

Let
Q=\big(I^{\operatorname{uf}}\subset I,\varepsilon\big)
be a quiver. Let
\widetilde{Q}=\big({I}^{\operatorname{uf}}\subset\tilde{I},\tilde{\varepsilon}\big)
be a quiver obtained from
Q
by adding frozen vertices labelled by
I^{\prime}=\{1^{\prime},\dots,f^{\prime}\}
and arrows such that
\widetilde{Q}
contains
Q
as a full subquiver. In other words,
\tilde{I}=I\sqcup I^{\prime}
and
\tilde{\varepsilon}
contains
\varepsilon
as a submatrix. Let
\big(\mathscr{A}_{|\widetilde{Q}|},\mathscr{X}_{|\widetilde{Q}|}\big)
be the cluster ensemble associated to
\widetilde{Q}
.

Following
[
33
, equation (3.5)]
, we define the following map

k\colon\ \mathscr{A}_{|\widetilde{Q}|}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tilde{p}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathscr{X}_{|\widetilde{Q}|}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle j}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathscr{X}_{|Q|}

The map
\tilde{p}
is a natural map as (
A.6
).
The map
j
is a surjective map such that
j^{*}X_{i,t}=X_{i,t}
for all
i\in I
.
The map
k
is the composition of
\tilde{p}
and
j
. It is surjective if and only if the submatrix
\tilde{\varepsilon}|_{\tilde{I}\times I}
of the exchange matrix
\tilde{\varepsilon}
is of full rank. In this case we get a natural injection

k^{*}\colon\ {\bf up}\big(\mathscr{X}_{|Q|}\big)\longrightarrow{\bf up}\big(\mathscr{A}_{|\widetilde{Q}|}\big).

The following easy Lemma generalizes one key part of the proof of Theorem
2.15
.

Lemma A.1
.

Assume
k
is surjective. Let
F\in\mathds{k}\big(\mathscr{X}_{|Q|}\big)
be a rational function on
\mathscr{X}_{|Q|}
. Then
F\in{\bf up}\big(\mathscr{X}_{|Q|}\big)
if and only if
k^{*}(F)\in{\bf up}\big(\mathscr{A}_{|\widetilde{Q}|}\big)
.

Proof.

Let
t
be a vertex of
\mathbb{T}_{l}
. Let
\chi_{t}=\{X_{i,t}\,|\,i\in I\}
and
\widetilde{\alpha}_{t}:=\big\{A_{j,t}\,|\,j\in\tilde{I}\big\}
be its corresponding clusters. Since
k^{*}
is injective and it maps Laurent monomials to Laurent monomials, we have

F\in\mathds{k}\big[X_{1,t}^{\pm},\dots,X_{m,t}^{\pm}\big]\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad k^{*}(F)\in\mathds{k}\big[A_{1,t}^{\pm},\dots,A_{m,t}^{\pm},A_{1^{\prime},t}^{\pm},\dots,A_{f^{\prime},t}^{\pm}\big].

By definition
{\bf up}(\mathscr{X}_{|Q|})
and
{\bf up}\big(\mathscr{A}_{|\widetilde{Q}|}\big)
are the intersections of Laurent polynomial rings. The lemma follows directly.
∎

Appendix B
Generalities on reduced plabic graphs

Reduced plabic graphs were first introduced by Postnikov in
[
26
]
. We briefly recall the definition and basic constructions of reduced plabic graphs here, and we mainly follow the convention used in
[
35
]
.

Let
D_{n}
be a disk with
n
marked points on its boundary labeled
1,\dots,n
in the clockwise direction. Let
\Gamma
be a bipartite graph embedded into
D_{n}
with a single edge connected to every boundary marked point. We draw
zig-zag
strands on
\Gamma
by drawing the following pattern around each vertex according to the color of the vertex:

Definition B.1
.

A rank
a

reduced plabic graph
on
D_{n}
is a bipartite graph with trivalent black vertices whose zig-zag strands do not self-intersect or form parallel bigons and go from
i
to
i+a
for every boundary marked point
i
.
9
9

9

This definition is equivalent to Postnikov’s original definition, but the bipartite and trivalent conditions help reduce the types of equivalence transformations to two.

Definition B.2
.

Let
\Gamma
be a rank
a
reduced plabic graph on
D_{n}
.
Connected components of the complement of
\Gamma
are called
faces
. A
boundary face
is a face that contains part of the boundary of
D_{n}
.
Let
\zeta_{i}
be the zig-zag strand going from the boundary marked point
i
to
i+a
. A face
f
is said to be
dominated
by
\zeta_{i}
if it lies to the left of
\zeta_{i}
with respect to the orientation of
\zeta_{i}
.
The
dominating set

I(f)
of
f
is a collection of the indices of zig-zag strands that dominate
f
.

It is know that every dominating set is of size
a
. The set
\{\Delta_{I(f)}\}
of Plücker coordinates with
f
runs through faces of
\Gamma
forms a
K_{2}
cluster chart on
\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}^{\times}(n)
associated to
Q_{\Gamma}
, where
Q_{\Gamma}
is the quiver determined by
\Gamma
as in Section
2.4
. For example, the cluster
\{\Delta_{I(i,j)}\}
associated to
Q_{a,n}
in (
2.21
) is defined in this way.

There are two types of transformations on reduced plabic graphs
\Gamma
called
2
-by-
2
moves.

•

The dominating sets associated to the 5 related faces on picture are of the forms

\displaystyle I(f_{c})=J\cup\{i,k\},\qquad I(f_{s})=J\cup\{k,l\},\qquad I(f_{w})=J\cup\{i,l\},

\displaystyle I(f_{e})=J\cup\{i,j\},\qquad I(f_{n})=J\cup\{j,k\},

where
1\leq i<j<k<l\leq n
and
J
is an
(a-2)
-element subset of
\{1,\dots,n\}\setminus\{i,j,k,l\}
.
The type I 2-by-2 move changes the dominating set associated to the central face to

I(f_{c}^{\prime})=J\cup\{j,l\},

and keeps the rest intact. Note that one has the Plücker relation

\Delta_{I(f_{c})}\Delta_{I(f_{c}^{\prime})}=\Delta_{I(f_{s})}{\Delta_{I(f_{n})}+\Delta_{I(f_{e})}\Delta_{I(f_{w})}}.

(B.1)

On the quiver level, it corresponds to the quiver mutation on
Q_{\Gamma}
at the vertex assigned to the central face, which locally is as follows

A type II 2-by-2 move changes neither the quiver nor the dominating sets of faces.

A result of Thurston
[
34
, Theorem 6]
can be restated in the reduced plabic graph language as saying that any two rank
a
reduced plabic graphs on
D_{n}
can be transformed into one another via a sequence of 2-by-2 moves of the above two types.
In conclusion, all the
K_{2}
cluster structures on
\mathscr{G}{\rm r}_{a}^{\times}(n)
defined by rank
a
reduced plabic graphs on
D_{n}
are equivalent.

Appendix C
Example of a reduced plabic graph transforming under
\boldsymbol{\rho}

Below is an example showing how a reduced plabic graph transforms under the rotation mutation sequence
\rho
. This example can be easily generalized to other standard reduced plabic graphs
\Gamma_{a,n}
with arbitrary parameters
(a,n)
. The example we choose to do is with parameters
(a=3
,
n=7)
.
